<image-desc>A printed bookplate from a collection of esoteric literature. It features a circular emblem with the words **HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY** <note>original Latin: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"</note> printed in gold. In the center is a blue oval depicting a pelican "in her piety"—a symbol where a mother bird feeds her young with her own blood. In alchemy, this represents the stage of "rubedo" or the nourishment of the "philosopher's stone" with spiritual life-force. The pelican sits upon a pedestal decorated with four roses arranged in a cross-shape. Rays of light shine down from above.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the thematic index of a treatise exploring the history and nature of magic and idolatry. # INDEX. §. 6.

The agreement and connection between Idolatry and Magic.

*The five arts of the devil by which he attempts to lead men away from God.* folio 36, side a

*Whether Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184 – c. 253 AD), an early Christian scholar whose complex views on the soul and the spiritual world were sometimes controversial</note> excused or defended magic, and his subsequent rebuke by Theophilus <note>Theophilus of Alexandria (died 412 AD), a bishop known for his fierce opposition to Origenism</note> for doing so.* folio 36, side b

*Magic as a second form of Idolatry.* folio 37, side a

*Orpheus <note>A legendary Greek figure known for his music and poetry, often credited with founding "Orphic" mystery religions</note> the magician, the primary author of Idolatry among the Greeks.* in the same place.

*Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, traditionally credited with establishing Rome's religious and political institutions</note> the magician, the primary author of Idolatry among the Romans.* folio 37, side b

*Numa borrowed a great part of his ceremonies and rites from Moses <note>The author here reflects a common early modern theory that ancient pagan wisdom was actually a corrupted or "stolen" version of earlier Hebrew revelation</note>.* folio 38, side a

*The Nymph Egeria <note>A divine figure or nymph who, according to Roman legend, was the consort and advisor of Numa Pompilius</note>, who taught Numa, was a succubus demon, not a righteous spirit.* in the same place.

*The Magic of Numa demonstrated.* folio 38, side b

*What the sacred rites of Eleusinian Ceres <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries, secret initiation ceremonies for the cult of the grain goddess Ceres (Demeter) held in ancient Greece</note> have to do with Magic.* folio 39, side b

*What the sacred rites of Serapis and Isis <note>Deities of the Egyptian pantheon whose worship spread throughout the Roman Empire</note> relate to Magic.* in the same place.

*That the Greeks were called "Ethnici" <note>original: "Ethnici"; from the Greek "ethnos" meaning nation, used by Christians to refer to pagans or heathens</note>, and not only during the time of Julian the Apostate <note>Roman Emperor (reigned 361–363 AD) who attempted to revive traditional Roman paganism after Christianity had become established</note>; against the view of Alciatus <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), a famous Italian jurist and writer</note>.* folio 40, sides a & b

*Magical schools existing before the birth of Christ.* in the same place.

*Which magic is said to have been permitted before the birth of Christ, according to Tertullian and Blessed Jerome <note>Two influential Latin Church Fathers; Jerome is best known for the Vulgate translation of the Bible</note>.* folio 41, sides a & b

*That the idols of the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum"</note> are actually demons, and that there is no other kind of demons, according to the opinion of the Dositheans <note>A Samaritan sect founded by Dositheus in the 1st century AD, known for strict asceticism</note> found in Photius <note>Photius I of Constantinople (c. 810–893), a prolific Byzantine scholar and patriarch</note>.* folio 41, side b

-># INDEX.<-

## Section 7.

Magical sacrifice: what it is, and how many types there are.
*The magical art: for what purpose it was devised by demons.*
*folio 48, side a*
How greatly the demons desire sacrifices to be offered to them, according to Saint Augustine <note>A foundational Christian theologian (354–430 AD) who argued in "The City of God" that pagan gods were actually demons craving worship</note>. folio 48, sides a and b.
*Demons are wonderfully delighted by sacrifices after the coming of Christ.* folio 49, side a.
*Iamblichus the Magician <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD), a Syrian Neoplatonist who defended "theurgy" or ritual magic as a way to commune with the divine</note> and his zeal for sacrificing to demons.* folio 49, side b; folio 50, side a.
The Devil appears in the form of a certain gladiator, or of Achilles, Hercules, or Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine</note>. folio 50, side b.
*How many kinds of lesser gods there are according to Iamblichus.* same place; and folio 51, side a.
A passage from Maximus of Tyre <note>A 2nd-century Greek philosopher who wrote on the nature of God and the role of intermediary spirits</note> explained. folio 51, side a.
*Iamblichus’s demonstration on the necessity of sacrifice.* same place.
The priest is the same as the magician, according to various authors. folio 51, side b.
*The nature of the distinction and order of sacrifice according to Iamblichus and Apuleius <note>A Roman writer and philosopher famously accused of using magic to win a wealthy widow’s hand in marriage</note>.* folio 52, side a.
What is meant by a "sacred man" <note>original: "Sacer homo," a person who, in ancient Roman law, was stripped of rights and "devoted" to a deity, making them a legal outcast who could be killed without penalty</note> and "scouring" <note>original: "Peripsema," a Greek term for refuse or offscouring, often used for a human scapegoat sacrificed to cleanse a city of plague</note>. folio 52, side b.
Humans sacrificed to demons in the New World <note>A reference to European accounts of human sacrifice in the Americas, particularly among the Aztecs and Incas</note>. same place.
*Why a human is such a pleasing victim to demons, according to Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a Roman scholar often cited by Augustine for his knowledge of ancient rituals</note>.* folio 53, side a.
*Enoch <note>original: "Chanochus," the biblical patriarch to whom many ancient books on secrets and spirits were attributed</note>, an ancient writer before the Flood, whose books on sacred matters and prophecies are among the Ethiopians...* <note>The Book of Enoch was preserved in the Ethiopic Ge'ez language</note>

### I N D E X .

Why the Pagans wanted their children to be baptized in the Christian rite, according to Isidore of Pelusium. <note>Isidore of Pelusium (died c. 450 AD) was an abbot and prolific letter writer who often addressed the superficial or superstitious adoption of Christian rites by non-believers.</note> folio 66, side a.

Parents rebuked by the Blessed Augustine for binding their baptized children to demons. <note>Augustine often complained that parents would seek Christian baptism for their children but then use pagan amulets or rituals to "protect" them, effectively trying to serve two masters.</note> in the same place.

How some inhabitants of the New World <note>original: "Noui Orbis Incolæ." This indicates the text was written after the European discovery of the Americas, likely in the late 16th or 17th century.</note> baptize their children, and the origin of that practice. in the same place.

That the Devil wished to be worshiped by the Blessed Martin, Abramius, and Valens the Hermit. <note>These refer to famous accounts of demonic temptation: St. Martin of Tours, Abraham of Edessa, and Valens the Palestinian, the latter of whom was famously deceived by a demon appearing as Christ.</note> folio 66, side b; folio 67, sides a, b, etc.

A passage from the Blessed Irenaeus regarding the worship demanded by the Devil. <note>Irenaeus of Lyons (2nd century) wrote extensively against Gnosticism and discussed the nature of the Devil's pride and his desire for the glory due to God.</note> folio 68, side b.

Divine permission is not a legitimate excuse for magicians and sorcerers. <note>The author argues that even if God allows evil to occur for His own higher purposes, the human practitioners of magic are still morally and legally guilty.</note> folio 68, side b; folio 69, side a.

On the punishments to be inflicted on magicians and sorcerers, and the distinction between these two groups. <note>In legal and theological texts, "magi" (magicians) often referred to those seeking knowledge or power, while "malefici" (sorcerers) specifically referred to those who used magic to cause physical harm.</note> folio 69, sides a, b.

The Novel <note>original: "Nouella." In Roman and Byzantine law, a "Novel" refers to a new law or constitution issued after the main codification of laws.</note> of Emperor Leo against magicians and sorcerers of every kind. <note>Referring to the laws of Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (reigned 886–912), who established harsh penalties for those practicing any form of magic.</note> folio 70, sides a, b.

This document certifies that the work has been reviewed by the theological authorities at the University of Paris and cleared for publication, a standard requirement for religious and philosophical works in the early 17th century.</meta>

## The Approval of the Scholars.

We, the undersigned Doctors of the Sorbonne <note>The Sorbonne was the prestigious faculty of theology at the University of Paris, which served as the primary authority for censoring books and ensuring they adhered to Catholic doctrine.</note>, certify that we have thoroughly read this book, titled *On Magical Idolatry* <note>original title: *De Idololatria Magica*</note>: in which we have found nothing that is contrary to the Catholic faith; indeed, we judge there to be many things within it useful for readers, for which reason it should be committed to the press and brought to light for the public good. Given at Paris, February 13, 1609.

->M. AUBRY.<-

->FROGER.<-

# MAGICAL IDOLATRY

## A DISSERTATION,

### BY JEAN FILESAC, THEOLOGIAN
<note>Jean Filesac (c. 1550–1633) was a distinguished French theologian and Dean of the Faculty of Theology in Paris, known for his writings on ecclesiastical discipline and law.</note>

->*of Paris.*<-

->To the most illustrious man<-

### NICOLAS CAMUS DE THORÉ,
<note>Nicolas Camus, seigneur de Thoré, was a member of a prominent family of magistrates and served as a counselor in the Parlement of Paris.</note>

->Royal Counselor in the supreme<-
-><term>Parliament of Paris</term>.<-
<note>original: "Senatu Parisiensi." In this context, "Senate" refers to the Parlement of Paris, the highest sovereign court of the French judicial system during the Ancien Régime.</note>

---

->§ I.<-

->*The reasons for writing.*<-

If anyone should perhaps wonder, MOST ILLUSTRIOUS CAMUS, why I am undertaking to write about the impiety of magic after so many and such learned writers have already done so, if the reasoning behind my plan is made clear to him, he will cease to wonder. Not long ago—at a time when all was peaceful and "Halcyon days" <note>original: "Alcedonia"; a term derived from Greek mythology referring to a period of calm and tranquility</note> surrounded the courts—you visited me kindly and graciously...

<summary>This title page and opening section introduce Jean Filesac's scholarly treatise on the relationship between magic and idolatry, dedicated to the high-ranking magistrate Nicolas Camus.</summary>

the matter arose; a conversation fell between us concerning **sorcerers** <term>Maleficis</term> <note>original: "Maleficis"; refers to those who practice "maleficium," or harmful magic intended to cause injury or death.</note> and **witches** <term>Strigibus</term> <note>original: "Strigibus"; literally "screech-owls," a term for night-hags or witches believed to fly at night and harm children.</note>, and when I said I could not stomach that this class of people—born for the plague and destruction of others—should wander so far and wide throughout France, or rather throughout all Europe, and should be seen to run riot with such impunity. In response, you pointed out that very many men of great reputation hold such an opinion on this whole matter as to say that far more things are found to be fictional and false than those which are discovered from the actual truth of the matter. We examined only two main points <note>original: "capita"</note> regarding these matters: one concerning the **transport** <term>transuectione</term> of sorcerers and witches to those nightly and detestable gatherings; the other concerning the sacrifice which is offered to that **Summanus** <note>Summanus was an ancient Roman deity of nocturnal thunder, frequently used by Renaissance demonologists as a name for Pluto or Satan in his role as "Prince of Darkness."</note>, Pluto, or Satan. Very learned men of our age have demonstrated that former point to be so true that if anyone should doubt such transport any further, he must be judged not only a stranger to the Christian faith and religion, but also as one barely in possession of his judgment and reason. Indeed, **Apuleius**—himself no amateur in that discipline—seems to me to have written truly about the nature of this kind of thing in the first book of his *Milesian Tales* <note>original: "Milesiarum"; better known today as "The Golden Ass" or "Metamorphoses," a Roman novel involving themes of magic and transformation.</note>: *That he has less understanding who*

### MAGICA.
->A ij<-

...by the most perverse opinions, he should think those things to be lies which either seem new to the ear, or unrefined to the sight, or certainly difficult beyond the grasp of thought; yet, if anyone explores these things a little more accurately, he will perceive them to be not only evident in discovery, but even easy in execution. Regarding this matter, we shall see later. 

Now, however, we must explain the final chapter concerning **Magical Sacrifice** <term>Sacrificio Magico</term> <note>original: "Sacrificio Magico"; refers to offerings made to the devil during a Sabbath.</note>. But, you will say, out of the number of those who have written about **Magic** <term>rebus Magicis</term>—and more or less twenty have written in our times, and among them the most powerful **King of Britain** <note>This refers to King James I (James VI of Scotland), who published his influential 'Daemonologie' in 1597.</note>—some have treated this very subject quite clearly and openly. 

But who would deny that after the harvest there is gleaning, after the vintage there are grapes left behind, after a **lavish feast** <note>original: "coenam dubiam"; a Roman idiom for a meal so abundant that the guest is "in doubt" as to what to eat first.</note> there are scraps, and after the forest is felled, remnants remain. Furthermore, those who have touched upon this argument have presented the matter as if it were plainly explored and understood, and as if held as an admitted fact, so much so that they did not wish to dwell on it any longer. 

But since it has been so arranged that this point—which ought to be primary and foremost in the inquiry held regarding **Magicians** <term>Magis</term> and **Sorcerers** <term>Maleficis</term>—is called into controversy by those who should have the least reason to doubt, for that reason, those things which...

# ON IDOLATRY

...matters omitted, neglected, or only cursorily noticed by others, I have deemed it worth the effort to explain more fully. This is not the place for me to speak on the entire category of **Magical matters** <term>rerum Magicarum</term>, nor to speak of them in a general way, even though I would gladly draw my pen <note>A metaphor for writing a sharp critique or attack.</note> against the men of this most unhappy age. These men have fallen into such madness that they boldly deny that any **demons** <term>dæmones</term> exist at all; they value any discussion of their power, capability, and works no more than the empty **dirges of professional mourners** <note>original: "Præficarum Nænias"; in ancient Rome, "praeficae" were women hired to wail at funerals. The author is calling demonology discussions "worthless old wives' tales" in the eyes of skeptics.</note> or the **funeral songs of Plautus** <note>original: "Plautique mortualia"; a reference to the Roman playwright Plautus and the ancient songs sung for the dead.</note>. 

But why is it a wonder if we have **Sadducees** <note>A Jewish sect described in the New Testament as denying the existence of spirits and the resurrection; here used as a label for materialists or skeptics.</note> in our own age, when so many are encountered for whom—to speak with **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) known for his fierce defenses of Christianity.</note> in his book *Against the Psychics*—*the belly is God, the lungs are the temple, and the stomach is the altar* <note>original: "Deus venter est, pulmo templum, & aqualiculus altare"; a scathing critique of people who live only for physical pleasure rather than spiritual truth.</note>. There are those of such a kind who, even if they do not deny God, clearly do not believe Him to be an examiner, or an arbiter, or a judge, as Tertullian writes in his book *On the Testimony of the Soul*. 

Indeed, I do not approve of those credulous sorts who would believe it is dark even at high noon if someone told them so; but in turn, I hate those **Pyrrhonists** <note>Followers of Pyrrho, representing extreme skepticism or the belief that certain knowledge is impossible.</note>, and those who refrain from asserting anything. I believe their hands are their only eyes, for they believe only what they see <note>An adaptation of the Roman proverb "oculatae manus sunt nostrae, credunt quod vident"—"our hands have eyes, they believe what they see." It suggests a refusal to believe in the spiritual or unseen.</note>. I would not wish [to rely on] the authority of **Apuleius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman writer and philosopher famously accused of practicing magic; author of the "Metamorphoses" (The Golden Ass).</note> alone...

<summary>The author critiques contemporary skeptics and "Sadducees" who deny the existence of demons and the spiritual realm. He argues that while some are too credulous, the extreme skepticism of his age is a form of madness rooted in materialism.</summary>

<keywords>demonology, skepticism, Sadducees, Pyrrhonists, Tertullian, Apuleius, materialism, atheism, idolatry</keywords>

### MAGIC.

...authority, likewise in the first book of the **Metamorphoses** <note>Referring to the *Metamorphoses* or *The Golden Ass* by the 2nd-century Roman author Apuleius.</note>, to affirm as certain truth that **witches** <term>Sagas</term> truly accomplish all these things: namely, to pull down the sky, to suspend the earth, to harden springs, to dissolve mountains, to raise up the **spirits of the dead** <term>Manes</term>, to cast down the Gods, to extinguish the stars, and to illuminate **Tartarus** <note>The deepest abyss of the underworld in Greek and Roman mythology.</note> itself. I add also that by **magical whispering** <note>original: "Magico susurramine"; refers to the low-voiced chanting of incantations.</note>, swift rivers are turned back, the sluggish sea is bound together, the lifeless winds cease to blow, the Sun is inhibited, the Moon is drawn down into foam <note>original: "Lunam despumari"; a reference to the ancient magical practice of "drawing down the moon," which was thought to produce a supernatural foam used in potions.</note>, stars are plucked out, the day is removed, and the night is held back. 

But that many of these things both happen and are perceived by the senses is taught not only by the reading of trustworthy writers—such as **Olaus Magnus** <note>A Swedish historian and cleric (1490–1557) famous for his "History of the Northern Peoples."</note>, **Saxo Grammaticus** <note>A Danish historian (c. 1150–1220) who recorded the legends of early Scandinavia.</note>, **Krantz** <note>Albert Krantz (1448–1517), a German historian who wrote on Northern and Saxon history.</note>, the chroniclers of **Northern matters** <note>original: "rerum Aquilonarium"; specifically referring to the history and folklore of Scandinavia and the Arctic regions.</note>, and others—but is even confirmed by use and experience. While these things are recounted on the authority of such great reliability, will men with such dull ears and stubborn hearts say they are being trifled with? But since that matter has been sufficiently examined, it is pleasing now to pursue the proposed argument, yet on this condition: that no fair judge of matters should demand from me a more abundant or more skillful dissertation than either my leisure—which those who know me realize is very little...

...know me, or my diligence (since it is so small, as I manage the matter by my own rowing <note>original: "meo remigio rem geram"; a Latin idiom meaning to handle a task by one's own efforts or resources.</note>, as reason allows). But since you have earnestly requested it of me, **Most Learned Camus** <note>Likely Jean-Pierre Camus (1584–1652), a prolific French bishop and author, or a similar contemporary scholar addressed in this dedication.</note>, if for a short while I deviate from the straight path I had established, or if anything seems not so coherent or elegant, let it be attributed first to the darkness of these matters—although quite a few flourishing in learning have tried to dispel those shadows with the brightness of their writings—and then to my hasty efforts, which will make you and those like you, the pillars and lights of good literature, more inclined to forgive. You know that saying of the **Florid One of Madaura** <note>Apuleius of Madaura, the 2nd-century author mentioned on the previous page; the quote is from his *Florida*, a collection of excerpts from his speeches.</note>: *Among wise men, judgment is usually stricter for finished works, but forgiveness is more generous for impromptu matters.*

## § 2.

->*The controversy proposed; the arguments against it scattered; in what way the Devil is bound.*<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'V' containing detailed floral and foliage designs.</image-desc>
So that our discourse may proceed hereafter by a certain path and order, the proposition to be examined must first be established. They say that after the saving birth of **Christ**, <note>The author is introducing the common theological argument of the "Binding of Satan" (from Revelation 20), which skeptics used to argue that demons and magic no longer functioned in the world.</note>

<summary>The author addresses a scholar named Camus, apologizing for any lack of polish in his writing due to the difficult nature of the subject and the haste of his work, before beginning a formal investigation into whether the Devil's power was curtailed after the birth of Christ.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the suppression of demonic influence following the advent of Christ. The author is addressing the scriptural basis for the "binding" of Satan and the historical shift in Roman religious practice from the reign of Constantine to Valentinian, arguing that while public idolatry was suppressed by the Church, the practice of magic survived as a secret, illicit discipline.</meta>

# MAGIC
A iiij

...no sacrifice was offered to the devil, with God so opposing it and Sacred Scripture proclaiming in **John 12**: *Now the Prince of this world shall be cast out.* <note>original: "Nunc Princeps huius mundi eijcietur foras"; a reference to John 12:31, interpreted by theologians as the moment Christ's sacrifice stripped the Devil of his legal claim over humanity.</note> It was necessary to add many prophecies from the Prophets concerning the utter destruction of the devil’s empire through the **Messiah**. Not long afterward, under **Jovian**, **Valentinian**, and the remaining Christian Princes, it began to be gradually extinguished, and was preserved nowhere except among those initiated into the discipline of **Magic**, as we shall say later. But let us now see what the true sense of this passage in the **Gospel of Saint John** might be. # ON IDOLATRY

[Regarding] **John, chapter 16** <note>While the text cites chapter 16, the specific phrase "the Prince of this world is cast out" appears in John 12:31. The author is likely synthesizing these chapters or referencing a specific commentary on them.</note>, he teaches that by the **name of the world** <note>original: "mundi nomine"</note> is designated here wicked and shameful men, who are subject and indulgent to perverse desires. He explains that **Christ** foresaw that after his Passion and glorification, many peoples throughout the entire world would believe; the **Devil** had previously inhabited their hearts and established his seat there. When these people were converted to Christ, embracing his faith and renouncing the Devil, then finally the Devil himself would be **cast out** <note>original: "eijceretur foràs"</note>.

Furthermore, the **Blessed Cyril** <note>Saint Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), a central figure in early Christian theology known for his commentaries on the Gospels.</note> asks whether the Devil had already been cast out from the hearts of the ancient righteous—namely the **Patriarchs**, **Prophets**, and those like them—and yet is said by Christ to be "about to be cast out" at that time. He responds that in the past, this had occurred in only a very few men, but now it was to be performed among many and great nations.

But, regarding what may be more relevant to our dispute, he adds another question: what is the meaning of the Devil being "now cast out," as if he were to tempt no one, when in fact he does not cease to tempt far more fiercely? He resolves this in a single word: that the Devil does not reign **from within** <note>original: "intrinsecus"</note> and in the souls as he did before, but only at that time from the out- <note>The text cuts off at the catchword "ex-", likely continuing as "externis" (from the outside), suggesting that the Devil's power changed from internal possession to external temptation.</note>

<summary>This page explores St. Cyril of Alexandria's interpretation of how Christ's arrival "cast out" the Devil from the hearts of the masses, shifting the nature of demonic influence from internal rule to external temptation.</summary>

**Blessed Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic known for his "elegant" or refined prose.</note>, distinguished for his piety and refinement of speech, elegantly illustrates this point by a comparison of similar things. *Perhaps someone might say: How is death seen to be overcome by the Head <note>original: "à capite"; referring to Christ as the head of the body of the Church.</note>, when it still rages with such great liberty against the members <note>The individual faithful people.</note>? If it is conquered, how does it still prevail over everyone, and there is no man who lives and does not see death? Death is clearly conquered...*

# ON IDOLATRY

the **body of the devil** <note>original: "corpus diaboli"; completing the sentence from the previous page.</note> and the punishment of sin? Sin, the cause of death, is conquered; the evil one himself, both the author of sin and of death, is also conquered. Not only are these things conquered, but they are also already judged and condemned: the sentence is indeed defined, but it has not yet been publicly proclaimed. Finally, the fire is already prepared for the devil, and even if he has not yet been thrown headlong into that fire, he is still permitted to act maliciously for a short time. Like a hammer in the hand of the heavenly craftsman, he has become the "hammer of the whole earth" <note>A reference to Jeremiah 50:23, often interpreted as the devil being used by God to shape or test humanity.</note>. He wears down the elect for their own benefit, but he crushes the reprobate for their damnation. As is the Head of the Household, so are his servants—namely, sin and death. 

For although sin is undoubtedly fixed to the cross along with Christ, nevertheless, in the meantime, it was permitted to **the Apostle** <note>Referring to Saint Paul; see Romans 7:17–20, where he describes sin dwelling within him.</note> not indeed to reign, but still to dwell within him even while he lived. So too death itself is by no means forced to be absent yet, but it is forced not to be harmful. From these things it appears what is meant by that **Dragon** <note>The Great Dragon of Revelation 20:2–3.</note> cast into the abyss and bound for a thousand years—that is, until the times of the **Antichrist**, as the Church Fathers explain. It is not that the faithful exist immune and secure from his harassment, but as **Blessed Augustine** says in Book 20 of *The City of God*, chapter 8: 

*The binding of the Devil is this: that he is not permitted to exercise the full power of temptation of which he is capable, whether by force or by craft, to seduce men and drive them into his faction...* <note>original: "cogendos"; the text cuts off mid-word at the end of the page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the devil's tactics against humanity, following a reference to the teachings or "precepts" of the Church fathers. The header "MAGICA" suggests this section explores the spiritual and supernatural mechanics of temptation and demonic influence.</meta>

He <note>Referring to the devil.</note> persecuted the Church in its infancy through Tyrants; he persecuted it in its growth through heretics; and now, in its joyful and flourishing state, he persecutes it through illicit movements. Three things reside in the soul: **Reason**, **Will**, and **Sensuality**. Judgment belongs to reason, desire to the will, and the flesh owes obedience. Therefore, he tortured the flesh in the **Martyrs**; he disturbed the reason in the simple-minded; but today he depraves the **Will** generally in everyone. 

He does not work with such open force as before, but with more subtle deceits and arts. He applies sharper and more vehement torches of temptation, from which even the elect are not immune—though the outcome is different and unequal for the wicked, upon whom (since they are like himself) he exercises a monstrous tyranny. This is elegantly explained in a few words by **Blessed Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose works shaped Western Christianity.</note> in the twelfth book of *The Literal Meaning of Genesis*:

> Under divine providence, the devil himself possesses his own desire to do harm, yet he has no power except that which is granted to him, either to subvert and destroy the **vessels of wrath** <term>Vessels of wrath (vasa iræ): A Pauline concept (Romans 9:22) referring to those who, through their own wickedness, are prepared for destruction.</term>, or to humble and test the **vessels of mercy** <term>Vessels of mercy (vasa misericordiæ): Those chosen by God to receive grace and salvation.</term>. <note>original: "Sub diuina prouidentia ipse diabolus suam quidem habet cupiditatem nocendi, facultatem autem non nisi quæ datur, vel ad subuertenda ac perdenda vasa iræ, vel ad humilianda siue probanda vasa misericordiæ."</note>

We cast him out like a raven, but he leaps in like a bee: and when we have recognized him in all these forms, he then transforms himself into a gnat <note>The author uses a series of animal metaphors to describe the "protean" nature of temptation—how the devil changes shape from something terrifying (an eagle) to something seemingly harmless (a sparrow or gnat) to find a way into the human soul.</note> and causes us no small harm.

Enough has been said thus far concerning the **bound devil** <note>original: "diabolo ligato." This refers to the theological concept based on Revelation 20, suggesting that after the coming of Christ, the devil's power was "bound" or limited, preventing him from deceiving the nations as openly as he once did.</note>—that is, the fact that his power has been restrained and repressed by the grace of Christ. <meta>This page continues from the discussion of the devil’s nature, moving from his deceptive transformations to a formal analysis of his inherent power and his place within the divine hierarchy of the universe.</meta>

# ON IDOLATRY

### §. 3.

->*The twofold power of the Devil and its nature;*<-
->*The Canon of Ancyra examined.*<- <note>original: "Canon Ancyranus." This refers to a 4th-century ecclesiastical decree that was a central text in medieval and early modern debates about witchcraft. It famously suggested that the "night-flights" of women following the goddess Diana were diabolical illusions rather than physical realities, a point of great contention for later demonologists.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'D' featuring foliate and floral motifs.</image-desc>No one with any wisdom would deny that the Devil possesses the greatest powers, whether from the excellence of his nature or his condition. There is that common saying from Job chapter 41: *There is no power upon earth that can be compared to him, who was made so that he should fear no one; he beholds every high thing, he is king over all the children of pride.* <note>original: "Non est super terram potestas quæ comparetur ei..." This is a standard Latin Vulgate citation of Job 41:24-25 (or 41:33-34 in modern Bibles), often used by theologians to justify the belief in the devil's vast influence over the material world.</note>

And indeed, the blessed Augustine, in Book 3 of *The Trinity*, chapter 4, demonstrates the extent of the demons' power based on the distribution of the universe itself through divine providence:
> *Just as,* he says, *thicker and lower bodies are governed by finer and more powerful ones in a certain order; so all bodies are governed by the spirit of life, and the spirit of irrational life* <note>Referring to the souls of animals.</note> *by the spirit of rational life, and the deserting and sinful spirit of rational life* <note>original: "spiritus vitæ rationalis desertor atque peccator." Augustine is referring to fallen angels or demons.</note> *by the pious and just spirit of rational life* <note>The holy angels.</note>, *and the latter by God Himself; and thus the entire creation is governed by its Creator, from whom, and through whom, and in whom it was also founded and established.* From that saying of the Blessed Au-

B

...Augustine’s opinion and similar views, our theologians recognize a twofold power in demons: one **intrinsic and natural**, the other **extrinsic**, which is considered in two ways: either if suitable material is not lacking upon which they might exercise their forces, or if no one opposes them. Just as feet are attributed to a human by nature, by which he is able to both step forward and walk; but furthermore, there is a need for a certain distance and space, and then that he himself does not live in shackles and fetters, or be held back by some serious disease. 

We see these same conditions in demons: if they find material suitable for accomplishing something, and if moreover no good Angels <note>In early modern theology, it was believed that demons were constantly restrained by "good" angels; without this divine "check," their natural power over the physical world would be catastrophic.</note> stand in their way, what will they not achieve and bring to pass? In Ephesians chapter 2, they are called **Rulers of the world** <note>original: "Mundi rectores"</note>. In Revelation chapter 7, they are said to **hold the winds**, and to have **received the power of harming the earth, the sea, and the trees**; and in chapter 14, to **have power over fire**. And the highest among them is called by the Apostle in the previously cited passage of Ephesians 2 the **Prince of the power of this air**; indeed, in 2 Corinthians 4, he is named the **God of this world**. Although—

<term>Demons (dæmonibus)</term>: Fallen spirits who retain their natural angelic intellect and power but use them for evil.
<term>Theologians (Theologi)</term>: Scholars of divine law and scripture.
<term>Angels (Angeli)</term>: Specifically "Good Angels" who act as the executioners of God's will and restrainers of demons.
<term>Rulers of the world (Mundi rectores)</term>: A title for demonic forces suggesting their influence over worldly affairs and the elements.
<term>Prince of the power of the air (Princeps poteſtatis aëris)</term>: A biblical title for Satan, referencing the belief that the "sublunary" air—the atmosphere between earth and the moon—was the habitation of demons.
<term>God of this world (Deus huius ſæculi)</term>: A title indicating the Devil's temporary dominion over those who prioritize worldly life over the spiritual.
<term>Ephesians (Epheſiorum)</term> and <term>Revelation (Apocalypſ.)</term>: Books of the New Testament.

### §. 3.

->*The twofold power of the Devil and its nature; the Canon of Ancyra <note>A reference to the Council of Ancyra (314 AD). Its decrees, specifically the "Canon Episcopi," were central to medieval and early modern debates about whether magic was a physical reality or a demonic illusion.</note> examined.*<-

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut initial 'D' featuring floral scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

No one of sound judgment <note>original: "nemo cui pectus sapiat," literally "no one in whom there is a heart [of wisdom]"</note> will deny that the Devil possesses the greatest powers, even by the very excellence of his natural condition. There is that well-known passage from Job, chapter 41: *There is no power on earth that can be compared to him, who was made so that he fears no one; he beholds every high thing, he is king over all the children of pride.* And indeed, the blessed Augustine, in Book 3 of *On the Trinity*, chapter 4, demonstrates the capability of demons based on the very distribution of the universe through divine providence:

> *Just as,* he says, *denser and lower bodies are ruled in a certain order by more subtle and powerful ones; so are all bodies ruled by the spirit of life; and the irrational spirit of life by the rational spirit of life; and the renegade and sinful rational spirit of life <note>referring to fallen angels or demons</note> by the pious and just rational spirit of life; and the latter by God Himself. Thus, the entire creation is ruled by its Creator, from whom, through whom, and in whom it was founded and established.*

From that opinion of Blessed Au- <margin>Blessed Augustine</margin>

<term>Spirit of life (spiritus vitae)</term>: In this context, it refers to the animating force or soul that governs physical matter. Augustine presents a hierarchy (the "Great Chain of Being") where higher, more "subtle" spirits naturally command lower, physical ones.
<term>Subtle (subtiliora)</term>: In early science and theology, this describes substances or beings that are less material and more spiritual, such as air, fire, or angels, allowing them to permeate and move "thicker" physical objects.

...gustine's opinion <note>original: "gustini sententia," completing the word "Augustini" from the previous page</note> and similar views, our theologians recognize a twofold power in demons: one intrinsic and natural, and the other extrinsic. Then there is that oft-cited **Canon of Ancyra** <note>A famous decree in canon law, often called the *Canon Episcopi*. It was long used to argue that the "Witches' Sabbath" and night-flights were demonic illusions rather than physical realities</note>, of which neither "head nor foot" <note>An idiom meaning it has no clear origin or structural integrity; original: *neque pes neque caput comparet*</note> can be found, whether in the published Canons of the Councils—for I do not care for the appendices—or in the body of the Canons. The Greeks do not recognize it, yet it is cited by **Burchard** <note>Burchard of Worms (c. 965–1025), a bishop whose *Decretum* was a massive collection of canon law</note> in Book 10, Chapter 1, and by **Ivo** <note>Ivo of Chartres (c. 1040–1115), a significant legal scholar of the medieval church</note>, and quite at length in Part 2, Chapter 30; then by **Gratian** <note>The 12th-century jurist often called the "Father of Canon Law"</note>, in whose work many things appear without an attributed author. This Canon provided all the material for doubting that transportation. A Canon of the **Council of Agde** <note>A regional church council held in Gaul (modern France) in 506 AD</note> also exists, cited by Ivo in Part 11, Chapter 54—for it is not listed among the published Canons—in clearly the same sense: 

*If there is any woman who says that she, with a crowd of demons transformed into the likeness of women, on certain nights...*

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the limits of magical power, transitioning from a discussion of specific sorcerers and legal canons to a theological explanation of why such wonders are permitted. # MAGIC

...let us explain. **Blessed Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose writings shaped much of medieval and early modern demonology</note> in various places, while he emphasizes the powers of demons, at the same time adds this precaution: namely, the permission of God. He teaches that they are not to be considered creators for that reason, even though they perform wonders, and he explains in what manner these things are done and for what purpose. 

For thus, in Book 3 of *On the Trinity*, chapter 3, after he recounted how the **Magicians of Pharaoh** <note>See Exodus 8:16-19</note> failed at the third sign—since they were unable to produce **Cyniphes** <note>original: "Cyniphes"; usually interpreted as gnats, lice, or biting flies</note>, that is, very tiny flies, as **Moses** had done—and then exclaimed, "The finger of God is here," he concludes: 

*From this it is understood that not even those transgressing Angels and powers of the air, through whom the magical arts achieve whatever they achieve, have any power unless it is granted from above. It is given either to deceive the deceitful, as it was given against the Egyptians and even the Magicians themselves, so that through the seduction of those spirits, those by whom the deeds were performed might seem admirable, while by the truth of God they were to be condemned; or it is given to warn the faithful not to desire to do such a thing as something great—for which reason these things have also been revealed to us by the authority of Scripture—or to exercise, test, and make manifest the patience of the righteous.*

Shortly after he adds: *Nor for that reason should it be thought that the matter of visible things serves these transgressing Angels at their whim...*

...let us explain. **Saint Augustine** <note>original: "B. Afterward, the **Blessed Augustine** compares demons with those who have been condemned to the mines <note>original: "metalla"; in the Roman world, being "condemned to the mines" (*damnatio ad metalla*) was one of the harshest punishments, second only to death</note>, whom water, fire, and earth serve in the metalworks and pits, so that they may do with them what they wish, but only as much as is permitted. > Therefore, just as we do not call parents the creators of humans, nor farmers the creators of crops—even though the power of God operates within to create these things while their external movements are applied—so it is not right to think that even the good Angels, let alone the evil ones, are creators, even if, by the subtlety of their bodily senses <note>Augustine often described angels and demons as having bodies made of "thick air," which allowed them more acute senses than humans</note>, they know the seeds of these things <note>original: "semina rerum"; referring to the "seminal reasons" or hidden potential within nature that Augustine believed God placed in creation to be brought forth later</note> which are more hidden to us, and secretly scatter them through the appropriate tempering of the elements, and thus provide opportunities for things to be born and for their growth to be accelerated.

But neither do the good do these things except as much as God commands, nor do the evil do them unjustly except as much as He Himself justly permits. For the wicked have an unjust will through their malice, but they receive power only justly, whether for their own punishment or that of others—either for the punishment of the wicked or the praise of the good. This final sentiment of **Blessed Augustine** was transcribed by **Blessed Gregory** <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), Pope and Doctor of the Church</note> and **Blessed Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153 AD), an influential Cistercian abbot</note>; the former in his *Commentary on Job*, book...

I do not investigate the fine details here—

and unhappiness [resulting from his pride...]

the shrewdness of the devil, who, not daring to lie when adjured <note>To "adjure" is to command someone solemnly, often under oath or in the name of God, to tell the truth or perform an action.</note> by a Christian, is forced to confess what he truly is; yet he impudently proclaims himself to be God among the Pagans <note>original: *Ethnicos* (the Gentiles or non-Christians of the Roman world)</note> themselves. # ON MAGIC

[the soul of a bea]st, or God. And certainly, as stated above, **Tertullian** consistently denied that the devil, when adjured <note>To "adjure" is to command someone solemnly, often in the name of God, to tell the truth.</note> by a Christian, would lie; yet he would never have dared to proclaim himself to be God in the presence of a Christian exorcist, a point which **Tertullian** emphasizes repeatedly in his *Apology* <note>original: *Apologeticus*; one of Tertullian's most famous works defending Christianity against Roman persecution.</note> and in this book, *On the Soul*. Therefore, when he says elsewhere that the devil proclaims himself to be God, this refers to his behavior among the Pagans <note>original: *Ethnicos*; referring to the non-Christian Graeco-Roman world.</note>; and **Tertullian** himself expresses this clearly enough in the *Apology*, chapter 23, when he says: *Let someone be produced here before your tribunals who is known to be tormented by a demon; if he is commanded to speak by any Christian, that spirit will as truly confess himself to be a Demon as he elsewhere falsely claims to be God.* <note>original: *Edatur hic aliquis sub tribunalibus vestris, quem dæmone angi constet, iussus à quolibet Christiano loqui. Spiritus ille tàm se Dæmonem confitebitur de vero, quàm alibi Deum de falso.*</note> The distinction was this: that the Demon—through exorcisms and, as noted above from **Tertullian**, by a "conquering authority"—was forced to confess his true nature against his will. # ON IDOLATRY

[He] claims the power of exorcising for Christians alone. The period before and after the birth of Christ is distinguished by this: that before Christ appeared in the flesh, the devil made use of statues or idols, and sacred ceremonies or rites, as well as men subject to him—such as **Magi** <term>Magi: Ancient practitioners of magic and astrology, often serving as advisors or priests in Eastern cultures.</term>, **Soothsayers** <term>Soothsayers: Specifically *Aruspices*, ancient Roman priests who practiced divination by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals to predict the future.</term>, and the like—to assert his false divinity. It was a fixed and certain belief among the **Pagans** <note>original: *Ethnicis*</note> that divinity resided within the idols and statues of their gods or demons.

This is taught by **Clement of Alexandria** in the sixth book of his *Miscellanies* <note>original: *Stromatum*</note> and in his *Exhortation to the Greeks* <note>original: *Protrepticon*</note>. **Eusebius** also discusses this in the fourth book of his *Preparation for the Gospel*, chapter 6 and in other chapters, as well as in the fifth book, chapter 2, and elsewhere throughout his work. Within the *Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca*</note> of Photius, chapter 215, we find that **Philoponus** wrote against Iamblichus’s book *On Images* <note>original: *de Simulachris*</note>, because Iamblichus asserted there that idols were divine and filled with the presence of a deity <note>original: *numinis*</note>. Indeed, in his book *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*, **Iamblichus** himself writes that the gods—which is to say, the demons—are specifically allotted various parts of the world, cities, houses, and statues. **Blessed Ambrose** also mentions this in his book *On the Mystery of the Lord’s Incarnation*...

[The devil] has borrowed from Jewish rites and sacred laws, and having fashioned and decorated them for a time according to his own manner, he presented them to his own followers. We shall speak of magicians and the other ministers of the devil later.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'A' in a square frame with architectural pillars and foliate scrolls.</image-desc> **Arnobius**, in the first book of *Against the Heathen* <note>Arnobius of Sicca (died c. 330 AD) was an Early Christian apologist. Original title: *Adversus Gentes*</note>, famously states: "It is characteristic, consistent with, and worthy of the true God to have bestowed—through the generosity of His bountiful power—nothing that is harmful or injurious, but rather that which brings aid, health, and is full of ways to help." Now, the methods he employed for the entire stage-play of his malice, and by which he falsely claimed divinity, are explained by **Tertullian** in his book *On the Soul* <note>A treatise written by Tertullian around 210 AD, often considered the first Christian psychological work</note>; he reduces them to three main categories: **Medicines**, **Warnings**, and **Prophecies**.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding how the devil establishes a false sense of divinity. # ON IDOLATRY

He borrowed from Jewish rites and sacred laws, and for a time presented them to his followers, decorated and fashioned in his own manner, as **Tertullian** <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155–220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage</note> records in his book *On Prescription Against Heretics* and in his book *On Baptism*. For this reason, he said that the devil emulates the things of God; and in his book *On the Testimony of the Soul*, he speaks of the "emulation of the truth by the unclean spirit" <note>original: *Aemulatio veritatis à Spiritu immundo*</note>, a concept also found in a well-known passage in his book *On Prescription Against Heretics*.

**Theodoret** <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), an influential theologian and bishop</note>, in his commentary on the *Second Epistle to the Corinthians*, writes: "It is the habit of the devil to imitate divine things."

**Saint Justin Martyr** in his *Second Apology*, **Eusebius** in many places throughout *The Preparation for the Gospel* <note>A work of Christian apologetics written by Eusebius in the early 4th century to prove the superiority of Christianity over paganism</note>, and also **Julius Firmicus Maternus** in his book *On the Error of Profane Religions*, further explain this matter.

**Saint Jerome**, in the second book of his work *Against Jovinian*, states: 

> The things that the devil does in emulation of God <note>original: *in æmulationem Dei*</note> do not prove our religion is a superstition; rather, they convict us of our own negligence, for we are unwilling to do that which even men of the world recognize to be good.

Concerning **Mages** <term>Mages: practitioners of magic, often viewed by early church fathers as the human "ministers" or agents through whom the devil operated</term> and other ministers of the devil, we shall speak later.

<summary>This page explores the concept of diabolical mimicry, citing several Church Fathers to argue that the devil deliberately imitates Jewish and Christian sacred rites to lead people into idolatry.</summary>

### §. 5.

**Arnobius** <note>Arnobius of Sicca (died c. 330 AD), an Early Christian apologist who wrote to defend Christianity against pagan charges</note>, in Book 1 of *Against the Heathen*, put it excellently: "It is characteristic, consistent, and worthy of the true God to have bestowed nothing harmful or injurious, but rather things that bring help, health, and are full of auxiliary means through the generosity of His munificent power." <note>original: "Proprium, consentaneum, dignum est vero Deo, nihil nocens aut noxium, sed opiferum, sed salutare..."</note> 

The devil knew this very well; therefore, he wanted to appear to have consulted and provided for the health and benefit of men through certain methods. Indeed, what those methods were that he employed for the whole stage-play of his malice, and by which he feigned divinity, **Tertullian** <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage, previously mentioned on page 21</note> explains in his book *On the Soul*. He reduces them to three primary categories: Medicines, Warnings, and Predictions. 

*Which, having been arranged to give the appearance of kindness and utility as if they were benefits, deceive all the more subtly, for while they are helping, they [distract] from inquiry*

<summary>This page argues that the devil gains a reputation for divinity by imitating God’s benevolence through "Medicines, Warnings, and Predictions." It cites Arnobius and Tertullian to explain how these apparent benefits are actually subtle deceptions to lead people away from the true God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of diabolical mimicry, specifically how the devil plagiarizes Jewish rites and sacred laws to create a false sense of divine authority. # ON IDOLATRY

He borrowed from Jewish rites and sacred laws, and having adorned and fashioned them after his own fashion for a time, he presented them to his own followers. This is according to the author **Tertullian** <note>Tertullian (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage</note> in his book *On Prescription against Heretics* and his book *On Baptism*. For this reason, he said that the devil emulates the things of God; and in his book *On the Testimony of the Soul*, he speaks of the "emulation of truth by an unclean spirit" <note>original: "Æmulatio veritatis à Spiritu immundo"</note>. This is also a commonly cited passage in his book *On Prescription against Heretics*. **Theodoret** <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393 – c. 458 AD), an influential theologian of the School of Antioch</note>, in his *Commentary on the Second Epistle to the Corinthians*, writes: "It is the custom of the devil to imitate divine things." <note>original: "Mos est diaboli diuina imitari"</note> 

**Blessed Justin Martyr** <note>An early Christian apologist and philosopher (c. 100 – 165 AD)</note> in his *Second Apology*, **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260 – 339 AD), often called the "Father of Church History," in his work *Preparation for the Gospel*</note> in several passages of his *Preparation for the Gospel*, and also **Julius Firmicus** <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Christian author and astrologer</note> in his book *On the Error of Profane Religions*, all explain this matter. **Blessed Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347 – 420 AD), the scholar responsible for the Vulgate Latin translation of the Bible</note> writes in his second book *Against Jovinian*: "The things which the devil does in emulation of God are not a superstition of our religion, but rather our own negligence is proven; for we are unwilling to do that which even the men of this world <note>meaning non-believers or those concerned with secular life</note> do not ignore to be good." 

We shall speak of the **Magi** <note>term: Magi; refers here to practitioners of magic, sorcerers, or those acting as ministers of the devil</note> and the other ministers of the devil hereafter.

### §. 5.

->*The arts of the Devil, by which he gained a reputation*<-

**Arnobius** <note>Arnobius of Sicca (died c. 330 AD), an Early Christian apologist who wrote to prove the truth of Christianity to the Romans</note> in the first book of his work *Against the Pagans* <note>original: *Adversus Gentes*</note>, stated most excellently: "It is proper, fitting, and worthy of the true God to have bestowed nothing harmful or injurious, but rather what is helpful, restorative, and full of auxiliary means through the generosity of his bountiful power." The devil knew this well; therefore, he wished to appear to have consulted and provided for the safety and benefit of mankind through certain methods. **Tertullian** <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD)</note>, in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: *De Anima*</note>, explains what those methods were that he employed for the entire performance of his malice, and by which he feigned divinity. He traces them back to three main categories: <term>Medicines</term> <note>Acts of healing or physical remedies</note>, <term>Warnings</term> <note>Divine admonitions or signs</note>, and <term>Prophecies</term> <note>Foretelling the future</note>. "These things," he says, "prepared under the appearance of kindness and utility as if they were benefits, deceive all the more subtly; for while they provide help, they [prevent] investigation..."

The devil draws [mankind] away from true divinity by the insinuation of a false one, and he causes all the more harm by helping. Let us now examine each point individually, and we shall begin with **Healing** <term>Medicatione</term> <note>The act of curing or administering remedies</note>, through which it is clear he has bound very many people to himself; and so that he might maintain this as his own special domain, he has even at times wished to have some dealings with **Magic**. Truly weightiest is that comparison made by **Arnobius** <note>A 4th-century Christian apologist</note> in the first book of *Against the Pagans* <note>original: *Adversus Gentes*</note>, between the divine works of **Christ**—who heals truly and in reality—and the demonic physicians of **Thessaly** <note>Thessaly, a region in Greece, was famous in the ancient world for its practitioners of magic and witchcraft</note>.

> *Christ, whether by placing his hand upon the weakened parts or by the command of a simple voice, opened the ears of the deaf, drove away blindness from the eyes, gave speech to the mute, loosened the bonds of the joints, granted the ability to walk to the crippled, and was accustomed to heal with a word and a command skin diseases <note>original: *vitiligines*</note>, shivering fevers <note>original: *querqueras*</note>, and dropsies <note>original: *intercutes morbos*; literally 'diseases under the skin'</note>, and all other kinds of **Ailments** <term>Valetudinum</term> which some unknown relentless cruelty wished human bodies to endure. What have all the [pagan] Gods done that is similar, from whom you say help was brought to the sick and those in danger? Who, if ever—as the story goes—they granted Medicine to some, either ordered some food to be taken, or a potion of a certain quality to be drunk, or the juices of herbs or grasses to be applied, or ordered them to walk or to rest because of the troubling causes, or some thing which [human] services...*

# ON IDOLATRY

...they feign <note>The Latin text begins mid-word with "-bos," likely completing "morbos" (diseases)</note> diseases, terrify minds, and distort limbs, so that they may compel people to worship them. Their goal is that once they have been fattened on the smoke of altars or the sacrifices of cattle, they may appear to have "cured" the ailments they themselves had inflicted, simply by loosening their grip. **Lactantius** <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD), an advisor to the first Christian emperor, Constantine, known as the "Christian Cicero"</note> imitated **Minutius**, his predecessor, using almost the same words in Book 2, chapter 14: *"Since demons are thin and incomprehensible spirits, they insinuate themselves into the bodies of men. Hidden secretly in the internal organs, they corrupt health, provoke diseases, terrify souls with dreams, and shake minds with madness, so that they may force men, through these evils, to run to them for help."*

While I am discussing medical matters, permit me—unless I am mistaken—to perform a "medical procedure" of my own upon the text of **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD) known for his rigorous and often difficult Latin prose</note>, specifically on the very argument being treated. The passage is found in his *Apology*, chapter 22, in these words: *"There is available to those demons, for the purpose of harming both substances of man <note>Meaning both the physical body and the spiritual soul</note>, a great subtlety and thinness from their spiritual powers; and although they are invisible and imperceptible, they appear in their effect rather than in their action."*

I believe these words, *effect* and *action*, have been accidentally swapped, and the text should read: *"invisible and imperceptible in their **action**, rather than appearing in their **effect**."* This is because the action of a harming demon—especially in the **Magical art** <term>ars Magica</term>—is more often silent and hidden, and thus invisible and in some way imper[ceptible]...

The following passages confirm my reading <note>The author is proposing a "reading" or specific editorial correction to Tertullian's manuscript</note>: *“If a certain hidden defect of the breeze casts down the fruit and crops while in bloom.”* And later: *“If the air is tainted by some hidden cause,”* and again, *“by the same obscurity of contagion.”* In no other way is this evil—whether proceeding from a sorcerer or from a poison—referred to by **Apuleius** <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), a Platonic philosopher and author famous for his trial on charges of magic</note> and others as a *“blind and inescapable destruction.”* <note>original: "Pernicies cæca & inevitabilis"</note> This point escaped even the most sharp-eyed and accurate translator of **Tertullian**.

It is therefore clear that **fictitious diseases** are inflicted by them so that, through a similar "cure," they may rouse the minds of men to worship and admire them. 

If anyone should ask whether they truly perform a work of healing, I would respond without any hesitation: those "remedies" for diseases provided by demons can grant them no reputation for divine power. For as **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian and exegete known for his work "The Preparation for the Gospel"</note> discusses in *The Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 4, Chapter 1: *“Demons know many things...”*

...[kinds] of herbs and plants, many kinds of spices and stones—unknown indeed to many—which either contract and condense the <term>humors</term> <note>The "humors" refers to the ancient medical theory of the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile—whose balance determined health and temperament</note>, or disperse and rarefy them by a certain natural faculty inherent in them. # ON IDOLATRY
->a<-

...as if they were characteristic works, belonging to the demons themselves, let us now see. I do not agree with **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), Roman author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note>, who wishes for **Medicine** to be a branch of **Magic**. I shall state why this does not please me after I have transcribed the full passage from Pliny’s *Natural History*, Book 30, Chapter 1.

"*No one doubts,*" he says, "*that **Magic** was born from **Medicine**, and that it crept in under the guise of health as something higher and more sacred than Medicine. To these it added the strength of religion through the most flattering and sought-after promises, which even now greatly blind the human race.*" That demons are the authors and inventors of Magic is only too true, and we shall explain this somewhat further later on. But to attribute the invention of Medicine to demons is plainly false.

For besides the fact that **Scripture** expressly teaches in **Ecclesiasticus** <note>Also known as the Book of Sirach, a book of the Biblical Apocrypha/Deuterocanon</note> chapter 38: *The Most High created medicine from the earth.* And indeed, "medicine" here is used not to refer to the medical art, but to **pharmacological remedies** <note>original: "Pharmacis"</note>; and the Greek text reads *pharmaka* <note>original: "φάρμακα" — medications, drugs, or herbs</note>. And likewise a little earlier, the **Vulgate** <note>The late 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> has: *For all healing is from God.* The Greek text has *iasis* <note>original: "ἴασις" — the act of healing or a cure</note>, meaning medicine or healing. It follows a little later regarding the same: *The Most High gave knowledge to men.* This reason alone proves that honor must be shown to the physician and the practitioner of the art, since...

He wishes the physician to be honored not with a simple honor, but with a manifold one. How truly, therefore, did **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher</note> write in Book 6, *On Benefits*, Chapter 16:

*Why do I owe something more to a physician and a teacher, and why do I not discharge my debt to them with a mere fee? Because from being a physician and a teacher they pass into the status of a friend, and they bind us not by the art which they sell, but by their kind and friendly goodwill.*

Nor does **Scripture** add "for necessities" in vain, implying that we may need the life-saving help of a physician not just once, but many times, and for that reason we ought to value him more highly. But if the Lord created him—which must also be understood as applying to the science of medicine itself—who would dare to attribute such a necessary and life-saving discipline (which **Isidore of Pelusium** <note>An ascetic saint and scholar from Egypt (d. c. 449 AD)</note> in Book 1, Letter 391, to the physician Domestius, calls a **philosophical art** <note>original Greek: "τέχνην φιλόσοφον"</note>) to **demons**, those most infectious and cruel enemies of mankind? As if...

# ON IDOLATRY

...to assign to [evil] authors, and to join with their destructive art. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist whose *Natural History* claimed magic was born from medicine</note> would have spoken more truly had he said that **Magic** sprouted from a curious desire for knowing or possessing hidden things.

For so **Blessed Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential Christian theologian</note>, in **Letter 5 to Marcellinus**, reveals that magical arts are sought only for earthly happiness, or for damnable curiosity. And indeed, in **Photius’s** <note>Photius I of Constantinople (c. 810–893 AD), whose *Bibliotheca* summarized many lost works of antiquity</note> account of **Philostratus**, **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker (1st century AD) often compared to Jesus</note>—who was the head of that school in his time—says that **Medicine** proceeded from divination; and not, on the contrary, as was the opinion of **Pliny**, who believed that a single **Magic** had reduced three of the most commanding arts of the human mind—**Theology**, **Medicine**, and **Mathematics**—into one, by embracing them all.

Nevertheless, I would not deny that **Magic** received its greatest growth under the pretext of **Medicine**, and that many sorcerers, under the appearance of practicing medicine, rubbed this stain of infamy onto this art. To the sick, whether because of the force of the disease or its long duration, the path to health through the usual remedies seemed long; [magical] words seemed more harmless and less harsh. **Plato**, in Book 4 of the ***Republic***, mentions that not only drugs, cauteries <note>original: "ustiones," the practice of burning or searing skin for medical purposes</note>, and incisions <note>original: "incisiones," referring to surgery</note> are employed to cure diseases, but also...

He mentions **Asclepiades**, a Physician and one skilled in magical matters, who revived the use of **white hellebore** <note>A poisonous plant used in antiquity to treat madness; its use required great skill to avoid killing the patient</note>, which had long since been lost. With it, he cured incurable diseases in an unusual manner, and he devised a mixture of various species to produce a breath resembling something lethal; at times, he was even seized by the sting and madness of a demon.

In clearly the same way, **Eunapius** <note>A 4th-century Greek historian and physician known for his "Lives of the Sophists"</note> records that **Ionicus**, an otherwise most excellent Physician and an outstanding inventor of the art of binding affected limbs so they would not be amputated by others, devoted himself to both types of divination. He practiced both the kind that, through the benefit of the medical art, obtains a presage <note>a physical sign or omen</note> of health in the sick, and the other kind which, by the instinct of Philosophy, descends and is spread among those who are able to guard and preserve it in a secret way. These last words signify the **Magical art** clearly enough.

**Apuleius** <note>A Roman writer and philosopher of the 2nd century AD who was famously accused of using magic</note> in his ***Apology*** truly declares: *"The ancient Physicians,"* he says, *"certainly knew that incantations were also remedies for wounds."* Perhaps what **Macrobius** <note>A high-ranking Roman official and author of the early 5th century AD</note> wrote concerning **Music** pertains to this matter—

Nevertheless, so that we may establish something certain about this whole matter, I will freely admit that formerly, not only among the barbarians but even among the Greeks and Romans, the use of those words and incantations <note>original: "carminum," which can mean songs, poems, or ritual spells</note> for curing or soothing diseases was considered harmless. 

Yet under the Emperors, it was occasionally permitted to use these things, though not without legal risk <note>original: "non sine noxa"</note>. I would add that it seems not so much the knowledge <note>original: "artem"</note> but the *practice* of the art that was prohibited. I will support this with the opinion of **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus, one of the most influential Roman jurists of the 3rd century AD</note>, in Book 7 of his work *On the Duty of the Proconsul*, under the title *On Mathematicians and Soothsayers* <note>original: "de Mathematicis & Vaticinationibus." In Roman law, "mathematicians" usually referred to astrologers and sorcerers who calculated people's fates</note>. Everyone knows that the term "magicians" is also included under those names. 

A passage on this exists in **Licinius Ruffinus** <note>A 3rd-century Roman jurist</note>, in the book *Comparison of Mosaic and Roman Laws* <note>original: "Collationis legum Mosaicarum & Romanarum," a famous 4th-century legal text comparing Jewish law with Roman statutes</note>, title 15, which the excellent **Pierre Pithou** <note>A 16th-century French lawyer and scholar (1539–1596) who rediscovered and published several ancient legal manuscripts</note> published—a man born for the benefit of our France and the liberal arts. 

Thus **Ulpian** says: *But it was questioned whether the knowledge of such men is punished, or the practice and profession of it. And indeed, among the Ancients, it was said that it was their profession, not their knowledge, that was prohibited; later, this varied. For it must not be hidden that sometimes the practice crept in where they even made a profession of it and gave public responses.*

Thus the most renowned **Pithou** emends the text. Meanwhile, regarding what they say, that the Emperors in former times did not prohi[bit]—

# ON IDOLATRY

...prohibited knowledge, but the profession; later, that same thing was allowed within the discipline of divination itself. Indeed, in the **Theodosian Code** <note>A collection of Roman laws compiled under Emperor Theodosius II in the 5th century</note>, Title on *Evildoers and Mathematicians* <note>original: "de Maleficis & Mathematicis." In the Roman legal context, "mathematicians" usually referred to astrologers and sorcerers who calculated fates rather than scholars of arithmetic</note>, Law 9, the Emperors speak thus:

> *We do not condemn the art of soothsaying* <note>original: "haruspicinam," the practice of inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals to predict the future</note>*, but we forbid it to be practiced in a harmful way.*

Therefore, under the Emperors, all that healing through incantations and ritual words <note>original: "carminum & verborum medicina," referring to spells or poetic chants used as medical cures</note> was more frequently forbidden. **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a highly influential 3rd-century Roman jurist whose writings make up a large part of the Digest</note> testifies to this in the *Digest* <note>the transcription uses "ff.", the traditional symbol for the Pandects or Digest of Roman Law</note>, Book 50, Title on *Extraordinary Proceedings*, Law 1, Section 2:

> *Perhaps one might accept as doctors even those who promise a cure for some part of the body or a specific pain—for example, a specialist for the ears, for fistulas, or for the teeth—but not if he used incantations, if he uttered curses, or if—to use the common word of impostors—he performed exorcisms. These are not legitimate kinds of medicine, even if there are those who claim with great praise that such things have benefited them.*

Ulpian, a Gentile <note>a non-Christian or Pagan</note>, lashes out at the Christians under the name of "impostors" because he adds that some were healed by magical chants of that kind. He asserts the same thing in Book 4 of the Code regarding *Evildoers and Mathematicians*, as many have noted. However, whoever used such sorceries <note>original: "veneficijs," which can refer to poisons, magic potions, or charms</note> to ward off diseases is called a *Medicamentarius* <note>A druggist or potion-maker; in this context, someone dealing in illicit magical remedies</note> in the **Theodosian Code**, Book 3, Title 16, on *Repudiations* <note>Laws regarding divorce</note>, Law 1; this is something the **Sun of Gaul** <note>original: "Sol Galliæ," a laudatory nickname for the French scholar Pierre Pithou, known for his vast knowledge of law and history</note> taught me.

->**Barnabé Brisson** <note>A 16th-century French jurist known for his work on legal definitions</note>, Book 2, *On the Meaning of Words* <note>original: "de Verbor. signific."</note>; and indeed **Andrea Alciato** <note>An Italian jurist and founder of the legal humanist school</note> puts it differently in Book 2, *Parerga*, Chapter 26.<-

Whatever the case may be, I am easily persuaded that pure, sincere medicine, consisting of legitimate natural remedies <note>original: "pharmacies," referring here to medicinal preparations or drugs</note>, has always been distinct from that superstitious and **Magical** sort. Whence **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a philosopher whose "Consolation of Philosophy" was a foundational medieval text</note>, in Book 1 of *The Consolation*, Prose 4, thus addresses Philosophy:

From this was born that legend about **Sylvester II**, Roman Pontiff—who was previously called **Gerbert**—whom they said was not ignorant of magical matters because of his skill in the mathematical arts. <note>Gerbert of Aurillac (c. 946–1003 AD) was a prolific scholar who introduced Arabic numerals and the abacus to Europe; his advanced knowledge led to posthumous rumors that he was a sorcerer in league with the devil.</note>

# ON IDOLATRY

...magic, **Hippocrates** <note>The famous Greek physician of the 5th century BC, often called the "Father of Medicine"</note>, the "greatest of physicians" as **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher and statesman</note> calls him, illustrated the art of medicine.

For what I speak of is said to have consisted of a certain daily exchange between life and death itself. According to **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a scholar whose "Bibliotheca" preserved summaries of many lost ancient works</note> in the *Bibliotheca*, chapter 166: in the most distant land of **Thule** <note>A legendary northernmost region of the world in ancient geography, often associated with Scandinavia or Iceland</note>, **Paapis**, having found **Dercyllis** (who had already arrived there along with **Dinia**), brought it about through his magical art that they would die during the day, but with the coming of night, they would return to life. To accomplish this, he used only the ritual of publicly spitting into their faces. 

There are two other categories which **Tertullian** <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD)</note> recounts, by which the devil passes himself off as a God among men: namely, warnings in dreams and predictions or divinations. Since these matters have been accurately explained by many others, there is no reason for me to dwell on them any longer, except to say that, in my own judgment, this whole story of dreams and apparitions, as well as the science of divining, is not only ridiculous,
