### MAGIC

...but for a while, let me show that it is sometimes even extremely dangerous. 

In the first place, regarding dreams <note>original: "insomnia," referring here to dream-visions or night terrors</note>, these include not only those which the demons themselves stir up in sleep, but also apparitions. By these means, those deceitful Spirits—to people who are not fully asleep or who have been startled from sleep—present a majestic sight, resembling the form of some divine power. And certainly, whether they appear in dreams or to those who are awake, and whether they falsely claim to be a God or a Goddess, or the persona of some deceased individual, they were accustomed to appear in a form **larger than human**.

**Juvenal**, *Satire 13*:
> Immediately the temple and the altars of the violated deity,
> And what especially weighs upon the mind with sweats:
> He sees you in dreams; your sacred and **larger-than-human image**
> Disturbs the fearful man and compels him to confess.
<note>original Latin: "Continuò templum & Violati numinis aras..." Juvenal (c. 55–138 AD) was a Roman poet; this passage describes how a guilty conscience manifests as a terrifying, supernatural vision of the person one has wronged.</note>

**Virgil**, *Aeneid*, Book 2:
> She appeared to me before my eyes, an image **larger than known**.
<note>original Latin: "Visa mihi ante oculos, & nota maior imago." In this epic poem, the hero Aeneas sees the ghost of his wife, Creusa; her increased size indicates her transition from a mortal to a supernatural being.</note>

**Tacitus**, *Histories*, Book 1: 
A figure **larger than human** had burst forth from the shrine of Juno. 
<note>original Latin: "Erupisse cella Iunonis maiorem humana speciem." Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD) was a Roman historian; he records this as a supernatural omen occurring during a period of civil unrest.</note>

The same **Tacitus** in the *Annals*, Book 2, speaking of **Curtius Rufus**, who was then stationed at **Adrumetum** <note>An ancient city in North Africa, located in modern-day Tunisia</note> in Africa, says:

<note>original Latin: "Oblata ei... species muliebris, Ultra modum humanum." This refers to a prophetic vision of the "Spirit of Africa" who predicted Rufus's future political success.</note>

# ON IDOLATRY

**Xenophon**, in the eighth book of the *Education of Cyrus* <note>original: "Xenophontem, de institutione Cyri libro 8." This work, also known as the *Cyropaedia*, is a partly fictionalized biography of Cyrus the Great written by the Greek historian Xenophon.</note>, records that while Cyrus was sleeping in his palace, a being appeared to have approached him that was more excellent and larger than human nature. It said to him: "Prepare yourself, O Cyrus, for you shall now depart to the Gods." To **Pisistratus**, the Tyrant of the Athenians, shortly before his death, something foul and of a form larger than human appeared, and taught him that no wicked or impious man would escape divine vengeance. 

Finally, **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote extensively on theurgy and Egyptian mysteries.</note>, in his book *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*, writes that Archangels and Principalities <note>These are specific ranks within a spiritual or celestial hierarchy.</note> only appear as very large and vast; it pleased Iamblichus, a devotee of magic, to name his **demons** <term>demons (daemones): in the Neoplatonic sense used by Iamblichus, these were intermediary spirits, though the author of this manuscript views them as deceptive entities.</term> in this way. These things indeed the demons did under the appearance of some Divinity; they also frequently did the same under the name of the deceased.

**Juvenal**, *Satire 2*:

> *What of the legion of Cremera and the youth consumed at Cannae,*
> *so many souls of wars, as often as such a ghost comes*
> *from here to them.*
<note>original: "Quid Cremeræ legio & Cannis consumpta iuventus..." Juvenal suggests that the spirits of Roman heroes killed in great battles would be appalled by the moral decay of their living descendants.</note>

**Virgil** can be seen to have aimed at this same idea in the fourth book of the *Aeneid*, where he introduces **Dido** speaking thus:

> *And now my great image shall go beneath the earth.*
<note>original: "Et nunc magna mei sub terras ibit imago." Dido speaks these words before her suicide. The author highlights the word "great" (magna) to support his argument that spirits—or the "images" of the dead—appear in an enlarged form.</note>

From this arose a proverb among the Hebrews: "Among the Giants," which is to say, "among the dead."

**Proverbs 21**, Vulgate version: 
*The man who wanders from the way of doctrine shall dwell in the assembly of the Giants.* 
<note>original: "Vir qui errauerit in via doctrinæ in cœtu Gigantum commorabitur." In modern English Bibles, this usually reads "assembly of the dead."</note> 
"Giants" here stands for the dead.

He notes from the Glossary of **Philo** <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher.</note> that the word **Rephaim** <term>Rephaim (Raphaim): an ancient Hebrew term that can refer both to a race of giants and to the spirits of the dead in Sheol.</term> signifies "Giants." See Drusius, *Questions*, Book 1, Question 37.

Now let us briefly examine the **art of divination**. 
Truly, who would not laugh at this—whether you prefer to call it a "science" or mere "conjecture"—as something utterly vain? Truly, this prediction concerning the **Ottoman Sultan** <note>original: *Osmano Sultano*</note> cost the **Hungarians** dearly. And indeed, **Constantius Augustus** <note>Constantius II (r. 337–361 AD), son of Constantine the Great.</note>, the son of **Constantine the Great**, quite rightly wanted a most severe inquiry into those "curious" people <note>original: *curiosos*; in this context, it refers to those who use forbidden arts or divination to pry into the future or the secrets of the state.</note>, even if they were members of the court of the Emperor or the Caesar. Subjected to the **rack** <note>original: *eculeo*; a wooden frame used for stretching the body as a form of torture.</note>, and with **iron claws** <note>original: *ungulis*; a Roman torture instrument used to furrow or tear the sides of the victim.</note> furrowing their sides, they were to endure punishments worthy of their own crime. ### §. 6.

->*The Agreement of Idolatry and Magic.*<-

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial letter 'S' decorated with human figures and leafy vines in a square frame.</image-desc>**SYMEON** <note>Symeon the New Theologian (949–1022 AD), a celebrated Byzantine Christian monk and mystic.</note>, the abbot of the **Monastery of Saint Mamas** in **Constantinople**, whose writings breathe an incredible piety, wrote more than seven hundred years ago in his 13th oration that the **devil** <note>original: *diabolus*</note> separates and leads men away from God through five "disciplines" or "sciences": **Paganism** <note>original: *Ethnicismo*</note>, **Judaism**, **Heresy**, the **Orthodox Life**, and finally the **monastic profession**, doing so through their contrary vices and corruptions. 

However, in which of these categories those harmful and superstitious **magical arts** are included cannot be stated in a single word. I would dare to affirm with certainty that for a large part they reflect **Paganism**, and they are not foreign to **Heresy**. For there are indeed some things in magic that savor of manifest heresy, but there is far more found in that branch of the discipline which they call **Theurgy** <term>Theurgia; literally "god-working," a form of "high magic" intended to invoke divine or celestial presences</term>, but which those who judge rightly call **Goetia** <term>Goetia; derived from the Greek word for wailing or moaning, it refers to "low magic," sorcery, or the conjuration of lower spirits and demons</term>. These practices agree with **Idolomania** <note>original: *Idolomania*; an obsessive or "mad" devotion to idols</note> and ancient **Hellenism** <note>original: *hellenismo*; here referring to the pagan religious practices of ancient Greece</note>. Let us now approach the matter more closely, based on the more universal principles explained so far, and let us investigate that perpetual agreement and fellowship between **Idolatry** and **Magic**, not only before the coming of the **Savior**, but also in our own times and even in these most recent centuries. **Theophilus of Alexandria** <note>Theophilus (died 412 AD) was the Patriarch of Alexandria known for his fierce opposition to both paganism and the followers of Origen.</note>, in his *Second Paschal Letter to the Bishops of all Egypt*, attacks **Origen** <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253 AD) was one of the most influential early Christian theologians, though several of his more speculative ideas were later condemned as heretical.</note> severely, as is his custom. Among the other monstrous opinions Origen devised, Theophilus claims he also granted patronage to the **magical arts**, since he affirmed that the "magical art" did not seem to him to be a name for anything that actually exists; or, if it did exist, it was not an evil work and therefore should not be despised. 

But while he does this, **Origen** shows himself to be a supporter of **Elymas the sorcerer** <note>original: *Elymae magi*; a Jewish magician described in the Book of Acts who was struck blind by the Apostle Paul for his opposition to the Gospel.</note>, who opposed the Apostles, and of **Jannes** and **Mambres** <note>Traditionally identified in Jewish and Christian lore as the Egyptian magicians who used their secret arts to challenge Moses before the Pharaoh.</note>, who resisted **Moses** with their magical arts. However, **Origen's** defense will have no strength, because **Christ** destroyed the illusions of the **Magicians** with His coming—at least among those Christians who are truly faithful. > After the **Gospel**, you will nowhere find either **Sophists**, or **Chaldeans** <note>original: *Chaldaeos*; a term often used in antiquity to refer to astrologers and practitioners of Babylonian occult wisdom.</note>, or **enchanters**, or **soothsayers**, or

<meta>This page continues the discussion on King Numa Pompilius and the origins of his religious laws. Having previously mentioned that Numa’s books were discovered buried with him, the author now addresses the chronological connection between Numa and the philosopher Pythagoras.</meta>

->### MAGIC. 39<-

I should advise that although these books of Numa are called **writings of Pythagorean philosophy** <note>original: "scripta Philosophiae Pythagoricae"</note>, no one should think that Numa was a disciple or follower of **Pythagoras** <note>a Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) famous for his mathematical theories and his belief in the transmigration of souls</note>. Pythagoras lived during the reign of **Servius Tullius** <note>the legendary sixth king of Rome (578–535 BC)</note>, roughly one hundred years after Numa, as testified by **Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BC–17 AD), a Roman historian</note> in his first book. Rather, they are linked because Numa was practiced in the same kind of magic and **occult philosophy** as Pythagoras; we shall see more regarding Pythagoras later. 

From these examples, it is clear enough that magic is both the parent and the nursling <note>original: "alumnam"; implying that magic is both born from and sustained by idolatry</note> of **idolatry**. However, **Saint Jerome** <note>an early Christian scholar (c. 347–420 AD) best known for translating the Bible into Latin</note>, in his preface to the *Epistle to the Ephesians*, suggests instead that the deceptions of the magical arts follow after idolatry. 

I would not wish anyone to think, however, that I believe the entirety of ancient idolatrous superstition consisted of, or was practiced solely through, the science of magic. This pestilential art was strictly forbidden, and to put it simply, I do not say that *every* form of idolatry was joined with the magical art; rather, I contend that magic *always* has idolatry as its companion. Certainly, **Saint Basil** <note>Basil of Caesarea (330–379 AD), an influential bishop and theologian of the early Church</note> indicates this clearly enough in his *Letter to Amphilochius* (Book 3, Canon 80). And although all the priests and overseers of the sacred rites were [devoted] to this...

# ON IDOLATRY

...then his mind was stirred by a poisonous potion, about which we shall speak in another place. What we have recounted thus far concerning **Numa** <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome (reigned 715–673 BC), credited with establishing Rome's religious laws</note> easily indicates what kind of man he was: namely, one instructed in the **magical discipline** <note>original: "magica disciplina"</note>. This can be proven quite clearly from the beginning of Book 5 of **Arnobius's** *Against the Heathen* <note>Arnobius of Sicca (died c. 330 AD), an Early Christian apologist who argued against pagan practices</note>, where a ridiculous dialogue is recorded between Numa and **Jupiter Archidæmon** <note>original: "Iouis Archidæmonis"; the "chief demon Jupiter." Early Christian writers often characterized pagan gods as literal demons</note>, after Numa had summoned him down to earth by the power of sacred rites. 

But to provide clearer evidence of this matter, let us hear **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar often cited by later Christian authors</note> from his book *On the Worship of the Gods*, as cited by **Saint Augustine** <note>the influential North African bishop and theologian (354–430 AD)</note> in Book 7, Chapter 33 of *The City of God*:

> That type of divination called **hydromancy** <note>original: "Hydromantia"; the art of divining the future using water</note> was brought from the **Persians**; both Numa himself and later the philosopher **Pythagoras** used it. In this practice, with the addition of blood, even the spirits of the underworld are summoned, and it is called **necromancy** <note>original: "νεκρομαντεία" (nekromanteia); the practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future</note> in Greek.

Could anything be stated more clearly to prove that we should regard Numa as a magician? This is in addition to what we noted in the previous chapter from **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman naturalist and author</note>, who himself described (citing the same Varro in the book just mentioned, as found in Saint Augustine) how Numa's books were discovered and subsequently **burned by the authority of the Senate** <note>The Roman Senate ordered Numa’s books burned in 181 BC, allegedly because their contents undermined the established religious order</note>. **Lactantius** <note>an early Christian author (c. 250–325 AD) and advisor to Emperor Constantine</note> elegantly attacks this same matter in the passage cited above. As for what Pliny says in the cited place—to mention it in passing—

<summary>The author uses classical citations from Varro and Augustine to label King Numa Pompilius a magician who practiced hydromancy and necromancy. He points to the Senate's historical burning of Numa's books as further proof of their dangerous magical content.</summary>

<keywords>Numa Pompilius, magic, hydromancy, necromancy, Varro, Saint Augustine, Arnobius, Pliny the Elder, Roman Senate, divination, idolatry</keywords>

# ON MAGIC

I should warn that, regarding those books of **Numa** which he calls the writings of **Pythagorean Philosophy**, there is no reason for anyone to think that Numa was either a disciple or a follower of **Pythagoras** <note>a Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) often associated with mysticism and mathematics</note>. This is because Pythagoras lived during the reign of **Servius Tullius** <note>the sixth king of Rome (reigned 578–535 BC)</note>, approximately one hundred years after Numa, according to the testimony of **Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BC – 17 AD), the premier historian of early Rome</note> in Book 1. Rather, the connection is made because Numa was well-versed in the same kind of magic and **occult philosophy** <note>original: "occultæ Philosophiæ"</note> as Pythagoras; we shall see more concerning Pythagoras later.

From these points, therefore, it is sufficiently clear that magic is both the parent and the foster-child of **idolatry**. However, **Saint Jerome** <note>the scholar and translator of the Bible (c. 347–420 AD)</note>, in his preface to the **Epistle to the Ephesians**, suggests instead that it is the deceptions of the magical arts that follow after idolatry. 

I would not wish anyone to think, however, that I believe every single superstition of ancient idolatry consists of or is practiced only through **magical science** <note>original: "magica scientia"</note>, since that pestiferous art was strictly forbidden. In a word: I do not say that every instance of idolatry is joined with the magical art, but I contend that magic always has idolatry as its companion. Certainly **Saint Basil** <note>Basil of Caesarea (330–379 AD), an influential Greek bishop and theologian</note> indicates this clearly enough in his third **Epistle to Amphilochius**, Canon 80. And although [he subjected] all the priests and overseers of the sacred rites to this...

<summary>The author clarifies the chronological gap between Numa and Pythagoras, arguing they are linked by their shared occult practices rather than a direct teacher-student relationship. He concludes by arguing that while not all idolatry involves magic, all magic is inherently tied to idolatry.</summary>

<keywords>Numa Pompilius, Pythagoras, Livy, Saint Jerome, Saint Basil, Idolatry, Occult Philosophy, Magical Science, Roman History, Early Church Fathers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that while not all ancient paganism was explicitly magical, many of the most important religious mysteries were deeply intertwined with the occult. It moves from general statements about idolatry to specific examples of Greco-Roman and Egyptian mystery cults.</meta>

# ON IDOLATRY

...it is in no way likely that everyone was initiated. Nevertheless, very many sacred rites existed among the **pagans** <note>original: "Ethnice"</note> which held the closest connection with **Magic**. So as not to be too long-winded in examining them, the sacred rites of **Eleusinian Ceres** <note>Ceres is the goddess of agriculture; her "mysteries" at Eleusis were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece, promising initiates a better afterlife</note> seem to have possessed something that savored of Magic. 

For what else could these words of **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar</note> mean, as quoted by **Macrobius** <note>a 5th-century Roman author</note> in Book 1 of the *Saturnalia*, chapter 16? 

> *When the **Mundus** <note>original: "Mundus"; a ritual pit in ancient Rome believed to be a gateway to the world of the dead</note> is open, it is open like a gate for the sorrowful gods and those of the underworld.* 

He calls those secret mysteries of Eleusinian Ceres the "Mundus," about which **Arnobius** <note>an early Christian apologist (died c. 330 AD)</note> writes in Book 1, as does **Clement of Alexandria** <note>a Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD)</note> in his *Exhortation to the Heathen*, and **Apuleius** <note>a Romanized Berber novelist and philosopher (c. 124–170 AD) who was famously accused of using magic to win a rich widow</note> in the first part of his *Apology*. To these I add the sacred rites of **Serapis** and **Isis** <note>Egyptian deities whose cults became immensely popular across the Roman Empire</note>. Apuleius, who was properly initiated into them, explains what magical elements might be seen there. In the second book <note>The famous description of initiation actually occurs in Book 11 of Apuleius's "The Golden Ass"</note> of his *Milesian Tales*, he recounts the things he saw during that initiation in these words:

> *Therefore listen, but believe what is true. I approached the boundary of death and, having trodden upon the threshold of **Proserpina** <note>the Queen of the Underworld</note>, and being borne through all the elements, I returned at midnight. I saw the Sun flashing with a brilliant white light; I approached the gods of the underworld and the gods of the heavens face to face, and worshipped them from nearby.*

And **Apuleius** himself, a **Magician** <note>original: "Magus"</note>, was a "priest of the province," as **Saint Augustine** says in Letter 5 to Marcellinus, and as **Eusebius** notes in his *Preparation for the Gospel*.

<summary>This page argues that the secret "mysteries" of ancient religions, such as those of Ceres, Isis, and Serapis, were essentially magical in nature. It cites classical authors and Church Fathers to link these religious initiations with occult experiences and the underworld.</summary>

<keywords>Eleusinian Mysteries, Ceres, Isis, Serapis, Apuleius, Varro, Mundus, Underworld, Initiation, Ritual Magic, Roman Religion</keywords>

# MAGIC

In Book 4, Chapter 1 of the *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: "Euang."; a work by Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian, defending Christianity against paganism</note>, it is written that several pagan priests were punished because they were sorcerers and magicians. Likewise, Nicetas <note>Nicetas Choniates, a 12th-century Byzantine historian and theologian</note> in Book 5, Chapter 20 of his *Treasury of Orthodoxy* declares that a priest of the Tyrian Hercules was found guilty of incantations. I also add Libanius <note>A magician mentioned in historical records, distinct from the famous orator of the same name</note>, who was killed by order of Placidia <note>Galla Placidia, a powerful Roman Empress and regent during the 5th century</note> because he was a pagan and an illusionist <note>original: "Praestigiator"; often used to describe someone who performs deceptive magic or "juggling" of appearances</note>; I have no doubt he was an initiate of the magical sacred rites. We said something about him previously from Photius’s *Library* <note>original: "Biblioth."; a 9th-century collection of summaries of hundreds of books, many of which are now lost</note>, chapter 80, in the section on Olympiodorus.

To this, I also join the Blessed Cyprian, the Martyr *of Asia* <note>Saint Cyprian of Antioch, a legendary figure said to have been a powerful sorcerer who commanded demons before converting to Christianity</note>, who, having worshipped idols with the greatest zeal from his childhood, easily attained the highest skill in magical matters, as described by the Empress Eudoxia <note>Aelia Eudocia, a 5th-century Byzantine empress and poet who wrote a verse life of St. Cyprian</note> in a poetic adaptation, which Photius mentions in chapter 184 of his *Library* under the entry for Eudoxia. 

And Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine monk and scholar known for his writings on the occult</note> in his *Book on Demons* writes that to the Greeks, Magic seemed to be a certain faculty possessing the greatest power, and was even the ultimate branch of the priesthood. Understand "Greeks" here not just as the inhabitants of Greece, nor only as the people who use that language, but as pagans and unbelievers. For in the New Testament, in Mark chapter 7, the Canaanite woman is called a *Greek* <note>original: "Ἑλληνίς" (Hellenis)</note>, that is, *a Greek woman*, not by her ethnicity, since...

# ON MAGIC

...professors existed before the coming of Christ, for it would not have gone unpunished if it were done openly and publicly in all sacred rites and sacrificial ceremonies. Although, on the other hand, <term>Tertullian</term> <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage</note> says in his book *On Idolatry* that Magic was permitted until the time of Christ. After he discussed the <term>Magi</term> <note>The "Wise Men" from the East; the author is debating their status as practitioners of a then-permissible form of astrology</note> who adored Christ and were skilled in Astrology, he adds:

> *Thus also there is another species of Magic, which works by miracles and, having once competed against Moses, was drawn by the patience of God all the way to the Gospel.* <note>original: "Sic & alia species Magiæ, quæ miraculis operatur & iam aduersus Moysen æmulata patientia Dei traxit ad Euangelium usque." This refers to the magicians in Pharaoh's court who performed wonders to match Moses's miracles.</note>

Blessed <term>Jerome</term> <note>St. Jerome (c. 347 – 420 AD), a scholar and theologian best known for his Latin translation of the Bible</note>, following Tertullian’s lead in Chapter 5 of his *Epistle to the Galatians*, said that Magic was forbidden by the New Testament. However, the truth is that Tertullian is indicating that <term>Astrology</term> <note>original: "Astrologiam"</note>—which is a type of Magic—was not condemned before Christ, and that it was permitted to interpret someone’s birth from the heavens. But as for that Magic which consists of <term>sorceries</term> <note>original: "veneficijs"; specifically poisoning or harmful magic</note>, the conjuring of demons, and other illusions, no one should persuade themselves that it was praised or accepted in use among Nations governed by better institutions and laws. Just as no one of sound mind would believe that Magic seemed to be permitted in the Old Testament, because to Blessed Jerome...

Blessed <term>Basil</term> <note>St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), a leading figure in early Eastern Christianity</note>, in his first *Epistle to Amphilochius*, chapter 7, joins idolaters with <term>sorcerers</term> <note>original: "veneficis"; literally "poisoners," but used in legal and religious texts to mean those who use harmful magic</note>. <term>Balsamon</term> <note>Theodore Balsamon (c. 1140–1199 AD), a renowned commentator on Byzantine church law</note>, in his commentary on this passage, interprets "idolaters" here as illusionists, who perform wonders by their incantations. 

<term>Photius</term> <note>Photius I of Constantinople (c. 810–893 AD), a scholar-patriarch known for his vast library and summaries of ancient books</note>, in chapter 230 of his *Bibliotheca* concerning Blessed Eulogius of Alexandria, relates that the <term>Dositheans</term> <note>A Samaritan sect often associated with Gnosticism</note>—having been taught by Dositheus the Samaritan, perhaps through a pagan error—asserted that idols or images are themselves demons, and that no other kind of demons should be sought. 

After Christ, even those among the faithful whom the "architect of all malice" <note>A traditional reference to the Devil</note> led into error, he taught them Idolatry along with Magic together with perverse dogmas. # MAGICA.

### ON IDOLATRY

"Flee," says <term>Firmicus</term> <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus (4th century AD), a Roman senator and Christian convert who wrote *On the Error of Profane Religions*</note>, "O wretched men, flee, and abandon that contagion with as much speed as you can; it is a Dragon that is worshipped. It cannot be hidden; he has himself confessed the property of his own name. Punishment follows a confessed criminal." The same author heaps many things in various places upon that Serpent.

There is an elegant mystical interpretation concerning the Serpent in <term>Clement of Alexandria</term> <note>Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150–215 AD), a prominent Christian theologian and philosopher</note>, in his *Exhortation to the Heathens* <note>original: "exhortatione ad Gentes"; also known as the *Protrepticus*</note>, when he says that the <term>Maenads</term> <note>Female followers of the god Bacchus, known for their ecstatic and wild behavior</note>—wounded by the flower of <term>Liber</term> <note>The ancient Italian god of fertility and wine, often identified with the Greek Bacchus or Dionysus</note>—howl in their Bacchic frenzy, "Eva, Eva!"—that very Eve through whom error followed—and that the sign of the Bacchic Orgies is a serpent initiated into the mysteries. He adds, moreover, that the name *Heva*, when aspirated, denotes a "female serpent" <note>original: "serpentem feminam"</note> in its Hebrew meaning <note>The author refers to a traditional etymological link between the name Eve (Chavvah) and the Aramaic/Syriac word for serpent (chiwya)</note>. Thus, among his own followers, the serpent—the most cunning of all living creatures—betrayed himself. 

But let us finally leave behind those <term>Ophites</term> <note>A Gnostic sect whose name derives from the Greek *ophis*, meaning serpent</note> and serpent-worshippers. <term>Blessed Epiphanius</term> in Heresy 27 and <term>Nicetas</term> in Book 4 of his *Treasury* relate that the <term>Carpocratian</term> <note>Followers of Carpocrates of Alexandria, a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher</note> heretics not only retained many of the delusions of ancient idolatry, but also followed Magic, and indeed with the greatest zeal, because they held magic schools among themselves in which those unspeakable <note>original: "nefandae"</note> arts were learned. ### MAGIC.

...in Book 4, heresy 42, [he] mentions certain Heretics whom he calls <term>Ethnophrones</term> <note>From the Greek *ethnos* (nation/gentile) and *phron* (mind); literally "those with a pagan mind." They were a 7th-century sect that combined Christian profession with pagan customs.</note>, that is, imitators of the rites and superstitions of the Pagans <note>original: "Gentilium"</note>, who together with Pagan Idolatry, employed prophecies and divinations, illusions and incantations. I do not ask here in a general sense whether heresy aligns perfectly with Idolatry and Magic; for to no one who possesses a modest heart can it be in doubt that these three plagues were born in a single birth, with <term>Orcus</term> <note>In Roman mythology, Orcus was a god of the underworld; here used to signify Hell or the devil as the "parent" of these evils.</note> as their parent, and that this has been repeated so often in our own age—that the leaders and princes of heresies have, either openly or secretly, taken counsel with their own <term>Vejovis</term> <note>An ancient Roman deity often interpreted as a "sinister" or "anti-Jupiter." The author uses this name to imply that heretics consult a dark or demonic power.</note>. I proceed and show among the faithful what were the remaining delusions of the ancient Idolatry that looked somewhat toward magic. The <term>Council of Elvira</term> <note>A provincial church council held in Roman Spain around 306 AD; it was one of the first to establish strict rules on Christian conduct.</note>, Canon 6, clearly asserts that Magic, even when it uses sorcery <note>original: "maleficio"</note>, is not without Idolatry. Chapter 8 of the <term>Council of Nantes</term> <note>A regional church council held in Gaul (modern France). While its exact date is debated, it is famous for its decrees against rural paganism.</note> is found in <term>Ivo</term> <note>Saint Ivo of Chartres (c. 1040–1115), a renowned expert in canon law whose collections of decrees were highly influential.</note>, part 2, chapter 38: *Bishops and their ministers ought to struggle with the greatest zeal so that trees consecrated to Demons, which the common people worship and hold in such veneration that they dare not prune a branch or a sprout from them, may be cut down by the roots and burned; also the stones which in the rui-* <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; "rui-" likely refers to ruins or remote places (*ruinis* or *rure*).</note>

## ON IDOLATRY
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

> "...[those things which] in remote and wooded places, <note>original: "notis/nofis locis & sylvestribus"</note> deceived by the delusions of demons, they worship, where they also make vows and bring offerings, let them be completely dug up and thrown into such a place where they can never be worshiped (or even found) by their followers."

This was indeed overt Idolatry, but how does it relate to the <term>malefic arts</term> <note>From the Latin *artes maleficas*, referring to harmful magic or sorcery intended to cause an effect through supernatural means.</note>? I observe that such remnants of pagan superstition for the most part were aimed either at remedies for diseases—granted, through the work of a demon—or at seeking omens and <term>divinations</term> <note>The practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means.</note>. This <term>Canon</term> <note>A decree or rule of the Church.</note> deals with the medical treatment of the sick, but specifically the kind that is sought from the devil. For it says in the following lines:

> "And let it be forbidden to all that no one should make a vow, or bring a candle or any gift to ask for their health anywhere else, except to the Church, to the Lord their God."

Chapter 6 of the <term>Council of Agde</term> <note>A regional church council held in 506 AD in southern Gaul; it issued many decrees aimed at maintaining discipline among the clergy and laity.</note> also points toward this, as <term>Ivo</term> <note>Ivo of Chartres (c. 1040–1115), a bishop and one of the most important collectors of canon law in the Middle Ages.</note> cites it in the same part, chapter 57; for it is not actually found among the official Canons of that Council.

> "It must be investigated whether anyone makes vows to trees, or to springs, or to certain stones, as if they were altars, or where they bring a candle or any gift, as if there were some <term>Divinity</term> <note>original: *Numen*; a Latin term for a spiritual power or local deity presiding over a specific place.</note> there that could bring either good or evil."

That also pertains to the healing of diseases, by an obscure and truly malefic art, mentioned in...

**Martin of Braga** in the *Collection of the Greek Synods*, chapter 74, states: *“It is not permitted when gathering medicinal herbs to follow any superstitious observations or incantations, except only the recitation of the Divine Symbol <note>The Apostles' Creed.</note> and the Lord’s Prayer, so that only God, the Creator of all and our Lord, may be honored.”* Of the same kind is that entry in the *Penitential of Bede* <note>A manual for priests to assign penance for specific sins.</note>, chapter 2: *“If a woman places her son upon a roof, or in a furnace, to cure a fever, let her perform penance for one year.”* <note>These dangerous folk remedies were intended to "smoke out" or transfer a child's illness through heat or proximity to the sky.</note> This is cited by **Burchard** in Book 10, chapter 14, and Book 19; and by **Ivo** in Part 2, chapter 41.

There is also a decree of the same type from the **Council of Arles**, chapter 5: *“Whoever attempts to employ diviners, enchanters, or diabolical amulets—or even herbs or ambers (otherwise called juices)—for themselves or for their family members...”* <note>original: "succinos, aliàs succos"</note> This is cited by **Burchard** in Book 10, chapter 33, and **Ivo** in Part 2, chapter 58. This passage is quoted in an incomplete form by **Bede** in his *Penitential*, chapter 2, and he omitted the phrase *“Cariagios and divining enchanters.”*

**Antonio Agustín**, a man most famous for his singular erudition, filled this gap from an ancient book. He teaches that <term>Cariagios</term> <note>A specific class of sorcerers or folk-healers mentioned in early medieval law; the term is often associated with those who interpreted signs or cast spells.</note> are also mentioned in chapter 14 of the (as yet) unpublished *Council of Narbonne*, where they are joined with <term>Sorticularij</term> <note>Fortune-tellers who predict the future by drawing lots or using "sortes" (tablets/sticks).</note>. He derives the word *Cariagi* from a Spanish idiom, and indeed among us French, the word *Cariage* is a term for...

# ON IDOLATRY

...as if for mockery and contempt, perhaps by that pestilential class of men who cannot be held in enough despise. Not only were some of the faithful deceived by the devil, using illegitimate remedies for health, but they also abused herbs and similar things—not without the secret help and worship of the demon himself—to the destruction of someone. From the **Council of Rouen**, chapter 4: *“It must be investigated if any swineherd, or oxherd, or hunter, or others of this sort, utters diabolical songs over bread, or over herbs, or over certain wicked ligatures <note>Magic knots or charms tied to influence events or health.</note>, and either hides these in a tree, or throws them into a fork in the road or a crossroads <note>original: "biuio, aut in triuio"</note>, so that he might free his own animals from plague and disaster while destroying those of another; all of which no faithful person doubts to be Idolatry, and therefore they must be utterly exterminated.”*

Likewise, the **Council of Ancyra** stated that solving one curse with another curse <note>original: "maleficium maleficio"</note> savors of Heathen superstition. This is found in chapter 24 (Greek) or 23 (Latin), according to the version by **Dionysius Exiguus** <note>A 6th-century monk famous for his collection of Church laws and for inventing the "Anno Domini" dating system.</note>: *“Those who seek out divinations and follow the customs of the Pagans, or bring such men into their homes for the purpose of seeking something out through magic art or for the sake of expiation <note>The act of making amends or purifying one's guilt through a ritual.</note>, shall lie under a five-year rule <note>A period of formal exclusion from the full life of the church.</note> according to the defined degrees of penance.”*

# MAGICA. 45

...that [canon] which he mentions, in the version by **Gentian** <note>Gentian Hervet (1499–1584), a French scholar who translated many Greek patristic works and church councils into Latin.</note>, is called the "discovery or purification of charms" <note>original: "medicamentorum inuentio seu luſtratio"</note>. I also add that Christians and lost souls have sacrificed to the gods of the Pagans or to demons, perhaps either through magical instruction or according to Heathen ritual. Thus, in the *Capitularies*, book 7, chapter 315, a priest is mentioned as slaughtering or sacrificing to the gods, and eating "idolothytes"—that is, meat sacrificed to idols <note>original: "Idolothytis, ſeu immolatitijs carnibus"</note>. In the same way, in the fifth book of **Greco-Roman Law**, priests are condemned who, according to Pagan ritual, used to sacrifice doves at the tombs of the dead. **Bede** <note>The Venerable Bede (c. 672–735), an English monk and historian.</note>, in his *Penitential*, chapter 7, records something very similar, which seems to refer to magic. *Regarding those who sacrifice to demons, we have the constitutions of **Bishop Theodore** <note>Theodore of Tarsus, a 7th-century Archbishop of Canterbury.</note>, in which it is written: "Those who sacrifice...*

# ON IDOLATRY

...to demons; for minor offenses let them do penance for one year, but for major ones, let them do penance for ten years. This is reported by **Burchard** <note>Burchard of Worms (c. 965–1025), a bishop whose "Decretum" was a major collection of canon law.</note> in book 10, chapter 23, and by **Ivo** <note>Ivo of Chartres (c. 1040–1115), an influential canonist.</note> in part 2, chapter 39. There is no reason for me to dwell on explaining the other two matters, which pertained to the taking of auspices <note>The practice of seeking omens from the flight of birds.</note> or divinations, for these have been treated abundantly by others.

However, I will only add this regarding divination which is written in Canon 60 of the **Council in Trullo** <note>Also known as the Quinisext Council (692 AD), it settled matters of discipline and ritual in the Byzantine Church.</note> and **Balsamon’s** <note>Theodore Balsamon, a 12th-century commentator on Church law.</note> commentary on this passage: that certain people existed in the East who would pretend to be possessed by a demon, and nevertheless, through this pretense, demons would become familiar to them. These people would use this art so that they might appear to prophesy in the manner of the Heathens <note>original: "more Gentilium"</note>, and by this means they would make a profit or seek fame. 

Therefore, I insist on this point generally: all these vain superstitions are always joined with magical matters and condemned under the name of Idolatry. For thus the *Capitularies* <note>The legal decrees issued by Frankish kings like Charlemagne.</note>, book 1, chapter 64, joins those who offer lights <note>The practice of lighting candles or torches at pagan shrines.</note> or perform other observations at trees, stones, or springs, with magicians <note>original: "Magis"</note> and sorcerers <note>original: "maleficis"</note>. *Capitularies*, book 6, chapter 210: *It has been decided that the faithful should beware of the vices that have remained from the ritual of the Heathens: that is, Magicians, Soothsayers, Fortune-tellers, Sorcerers, Diviners, Enchanters, Dream-interpreters, and Omen-readers.* <note>original: "Magi, Arioli, Sortilegi, Venefici, Diuini, Incantatores, Somniatores, Coniectores"</note>

# MAGIC MATTERS

...those whom divine law commands to be punished irrevocably. In Greco-Roman law, in Book 5 of the *Responses* of Nicetas, Archbishop of Thessaloniki <note>Nicetas of Thessaloniki was a 12th-century Byzantine prelate known for his expertise in canon law.</note>, where he deals with **Enchanters**, **Poisoners** <note>original: "Veneficis"; in this context, it refers to both physical poisoners and those who use harmful sorcery.</note>, and **Mountebanks** <note>original: "Circulatoribus"; itinerant performers or healers who used "magic" to deceive people for money.</note>, and the various punishments—to be enforced by the Church according to each person's status—he responds that these are **Pagan customs**, and are not to be tolerated in a Christian Republic, and even less so in the Catholic Church. 

That most impure Cornelius <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous Renaissance occultist who later wrote "De incertitudine et vanitate scientiarum" (On the Uncertainty and Vanity of the Sciences) in 1526, criticizing the very arts he once practiced.</note>, the prince of the school of magic, in Book 1 of *On the Vanity of Sciences*, chapter 48, calls all these things the frauds of **Idolatry**. 

But to conclude this entire dispute about the Idolatry detected in Magic briefly and clearly, we must explain in how many and in what ways magi and sorcerers—and in a single word, those who use these arts—offer <term>latria</term> <note>The highest form of worship, which theology teaches is reserved for God alone.</note> and supreme cult to the devil, no differently than to God. 

Those various ways are reviewed by **Eymeric** <note>Nicholas Eymeric (c. 1320–1399), a Spanish Inquisitor whose "Directorium Inquisitorum" became the standard manual for the Inquisition.</note>, who lived a hundred years ago, in his *Directory of Inquisitors*, part 3, question 43. He writes as follows: 

*Certain people, in invoking demons, clearly exhibit the honor of divine worship to the invoked demons, such as by sacrificing to them, worshipping them, pouring out execrable prayers, devoting themselves to the demons, obeying...*

# MAGIC.

...may agree. I shall conclude this section concerning the fellowship and—to speak more truly—the brotherhood of Magic and Idolatry with a charming and graceful story, which Marco Polo <note>The text refers to him as *Paulus Venetus*, the Latinized name for the famous Venetian explorer Marco Polo.</note> relates in the final chapter of the first book, and the second chapter of the second book of his *Travels* <note>original: *Peregrinationum*; Polo's account was widely circulated in various Latin and vernacular editions.</note> in the Venice edition (for the Basel edition is organized differently). The priests of that Great Emperor of the Tartars <note>A historical term for the Mongols and related nomadic peoples of Central and East Asia.</note>—who are both idolaters and magicians—ensure by their magic art that when the Prince sits at the table, golden bowls and cups filled with pure wine are transported through the air from another table to the Emperor himself. This occurs without any of the servants assisting or even touching them. Once he has drunk, those cups soon return by their own power—or rather, the power of demons—to the previous table which is positioned in the middle of the hall. 

Now, when that Prince—who was then called Kublai Khan <note>The founder of the Yuan dynasty in China and grandson of Genghis Khan.</note>, or Cobila, as Hayton the Armenian <note>A medieval monk and historian who wrote about the Mongol Empire.</note> calls him in his history—was somewhat inclined toward the Christian religion, he was asked by Niccolò and Maffeo Polo <note>The father and uncle of Marco Polo, who traveled to the Mongol court before Marco's famous journey.</note> (whom he later sent as ambassadors to the Supreme Pontiff <note>The Pope.</note>) why he did not embrace the faith of Christ. He responded that his priests performed wonderful and stu- <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the word is likely "stupendous."</note>

### §. 7.

### *Magic Sacrifice: what it is and its various kinds.*

I have never approved of the opinion of those, nor can I ever approve of it, who greatly downplay the devil’s impiety when they deny that the demon claims for himself the title of divinity among his followers, or that he wishes men to think so highly of him as if he were some divine Power <note>original: *Numen*; a Latin term for a deity or a presiding divine spirit.</note>, and thus [comparing] him with God...

# MAGICA.
### 48

...they arrange matters, as if some literate slave, while looking upon his master, thinks more of his own physical strength and power, or of the place where dead oxen strike living men <note>original: *vbi viuos homines mortui incursant boues*. This is a classical riddle or metaphor for a whip made of leather (ox-hide) used to beat slaves.</note>, than about seeking to follow his master’s command. Most truly the Royal Prophet <note>King David, traditionally considered the author of the Psalms.</note> says in Psalm 73: *The pride of those who hate you rises up always.* <note>original: *Superbia eorum qui te oderunt ascendit semper.*</note> Eusebius, in *The Preparation for the Gospel* <note>A 4th-century work by Eusebius of Caesarea defending Christianity against paganism.</note>, book 4, chapter 2, most clearly testifies that demons devised the malevolent arts and many obscene things for this end: so that men, enticed by these things, might think the Prince and Arch-demon of these spirits was the supreme God. To explain this more clearly, let us examine this very thing in that highest point of all divine worship, which rests upon **sacrifice**. Blessed Augustine, in Book 10 of *The City of God*, chapter 7, assigns this distinction between good and evil angels: that those <term>Eudaemons: from the Greek *eudaimon*, meaning a good or "well-fated" spirit, as opposed to a "kakodaemon" or evil spirit.</term> and honest angels:

> *Because they mercifully love us—mortal and wretched as we are—so that we might be immortal and blessed, they do not want us to sacrifice to them, but rather to Him whose sacrifice they know themselves to be, along with us.* <note>original: *Quoniam, nos mortales & miseros... nolunt nos sibi sacrificare, sed ei cuius & ipsi nobiscum sacrificium se esse nouerunt.*</note>

There is also a noble question by the same Blessed Augustine in chapter 16 of the same book:

> *Whether those should be believed who wish to be worshipped with religious rites, demanding sacred things and sacrifices for themselves from...* <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

# ON IDOLATRY

...to be offered to mortals, or to those who say that all this worship is owed to the one God, the creator of all things, and who command with true piety that it be rendered to Him, in whose contemplation they themselves are blessed, and promise that we shall be so likewise. A little later, he says: Let them respond whether sacrifice should be made to the Gods or Angels who command it to be sacrificed to themselves, or to that One to whom those command it, who forbid it both to themselves and to those others. <note>original: *Paulò post. Respondeant, ait, an sacrificandum sit Dijs vel Angelis...* This is a paraphrase from Augustine's City of God, questioning why one would worship lesser beings who demand it, rather than the one God whom the holy angels point toward.</note>

But indeed, in chapter 19 of the same book, after he has taught that a visible sacrifice must not be offered to any other than to Him, whose invisible sacrifice we ourselves ought to be in our hearts. After a while he adds: *But those deceitful demons proudly demand this duty of sacrifice for themselves for no other reason than because they know it is owed to the True God.* For they do not truly—as *Porphyry* <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher and a prominent critic of early Christianity.</note> and some others think—rejoice in the stench of carcasses <note>original: *cadauerinis nidoribus*. This refers to the smoke and smell of animal sacrifices common in pagan rituals.</note>, but in divine honors. Therefore, those spirits who claim divinity for themselves are delighted not by the smoke of any physical body, but by the soul of the supplicant, over whom they might rule once he is deceived and subjected, blocking the path to the true God, so that man might not be His sacrifice while he sacrifices to someone other than Him. 

But let us weigh each point separately, and first: Blessed Augustine designates here for us a twofold sacrifice: one visible, external, and as it were physical; the other spiritual and mystical. Of the former...

For according to **Apuleius** <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD) was a Roman philosopher and author famous for his work on magic and his novel *The Golden Ass*.</note>, in the language of the Persians, a *Mage* is what in our language—that is, Latin—is a **Priest**: one who rightly knows, understands, and is expert in the laws of ceremonies, the divine law of sacred rites, and the justice of religions. These are his words in his *Apology* <note>Apuleius's *Apologia* was his legal defense against the charge of using magic to win a wealthy widow's hand in marriage.</note>; he says that for **Plato** in the first *Alcibiades*, "Magic" signified nothing other than the *worship of the Gods* <note>original: θεῶν θεραπείαν (*theōn therapeian*)</note>.

And that the Mages of the Persians are in reality Priests, as Apuleius indicated above, is declared by **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus (c. 1256–1335), the last of the great Greek ecclesiastical historians.</note> in the 8th book of his *Ecclesiastical History*, chapter 36. Others testify to the same, whom it is not necessary to bring forward now.

...as Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was one of the last major Greek Neoplatonist philosophers, who wrote extensively on how ritual and prayer connect the soul to the divine.</note> reports in his book *On Sacrifice and Magic*. Many learned men have explained what a **Sacred-man** <term>Sacer-homo: in Roman law, a person who was cursed and excluded from society, who could be killed without the killer being charged with murder, often functioning as a ritual scapegoat</term> might be, and the Blessed Paul denotes this in 1 Corinthians 4: "*We have been made the refuse of all things until now*" <note>original Latin: *Facti sumus omnium peripsema vsque adhuc*</note>. Others read the word "purification-offering" <note>original Greek: *κάθαρμα* (katharma), referring to a person or object used in a ritual cleansing of a city</note> in the Apostle’s text, conveying the same sense. By the Apostle, the **Sacred-man** and the **offscouring** <note>original Latin: *peripsema*</note> refer to one who annually atoned for the sins of the people and was thrown into the sea as a sacrificial victim to **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea.</note>. Immediately afterward, those present would cry out: "Be our cleansing," or "Be our health and redemption." I have transcribed this from **Drusius** <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616) was a Flemish Protestant scholar and expert in Hebrew and ancient proverbs.</note>, from his *Proverbs*, Class 2, book 4. **Suidas** <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary.</note> explains this under the term "offscouring" <note>original Greek: *περίψημα* (peripsēma)</note>, as does **Budé** <note>Guillaume Budé (1467–1540), a preeminent French humanist and scholar.</note>, the greatest glory of France and of our **Paris** <note>original Latin: *Lutetiæ*</note>.

But indeed, that most hostile enemy of mankind <note>A common reference to the Devil or Satan.</note> was not only delighted by these lustratory <note>purifying</note> and human victims, but also by other humans slaughtered at impure altars. Secular writers have much to say on this matter, as does **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a Christian historian who documented the early Church and criticized pagan customs.</note> in his *Preparation for the Gospel* in various places, especially in Book 4, Chapter 7, and Book 5, Chapter 6—to say nothing of other sacred authors. Furthermore, in our own age, **Peter Martyr of Anghiera** <note>original: *Petrus Martyr Mediolanensis* (1457–1526). ### MAGIC.

[Porphyry] elegantly describes the tricks and deceptions of demons in his book *On Sacrifices* <note>original: "De Sacrificijs"</note>, when he reveals that demons lead us to offer prayers and perform sacred rites to the beneficial Gods, as if those Gods were angry with us. Indeed, they are such "skin-shifters" <note>original: "versipelles"; refers to shape-shifting or deceptive nature</note> that, through wondrous illusions, they frequently present themselves as the Gods whom people believe to be good and kind, or they take on another mask entirely. He says that they perform these and similar things because they are eager to lead us away from the right understanding of the Gods and instead draw us toward themselves.

**Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher and student of Porphyry</note>, following his teacher, reports the same thing: namely, that in <term>Theurgy: a practice involving rituals to invoke the presence of gods or spirits</term>—which is to say, a god-working operation and the art of sacrifice pertaining to the favorable and kind Gods—if even a slight error is made, then it is not the good spirits <note>original: "Numina"</note> who were desired that appear, but rather worse and harmful ones. These appear in the likeness and form of the good ones. It commonly happens that those wicked spirits transform themselves into the resemblance of good spirits and appear haughty, claiming for themselves more power than they actually possess.

To these accounts I will join a closely related passage from **Arnobius**, book 4: *“If the Magi, who are the brothers of the <term>Haruspexes: priests in ancient Rome who interpreted omens from the entrails of sacrificed animals</term>, mention in their own rituals that <term>Antitheos: opposing spirits or "anti-gods" who mimic the divine</term> often creep in in place of the true gods; and that these are certain spirits made from coarser matter who pretend to be Gods...”*

Meanwhile, one may understand those magical expiations <note>original: "expiationes illas Magicas"</note> of which **Proclus** <note>A 5th-century Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and the mystical properties of rituals.</note> writes, which the **Magi** themselves call ***Teletæ*** <note>original: "Teletæ"; from the Greek, referring to "perfecting" or "initiatory" rites intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note>. These are discussed in the cited books of **Iamblichus** and **Porphyry**, in the second book of the *Milesian Tales* <note>original: "Milesiarum"; another name for The Golden Ass by Apuleius, a Roman novel famous for its depictions of witchcraft.</note> by **Apuleius**, as well as by **Varro** in the writings of **Saint Augustine** in the fourth book of *The City of God*, chapter 31. Similarly, **Plato** mentions them in the second book of the *Republic*, attributing their invention to **Orpheus**, **Linus**, and similar ancient Magi.

Let us proceed further and recognize the dedication of the remaining Magi in offering sacrifices to **Demons** after the birth of Christ. Although **Eunapius** <note>A 4th-century historian whose "Lives of the Philosophers" is a primary source for the history of Neoplatonism and late-antique paganism.</note> provides very many examples of this matter everywhere—all of which I would seem to be entirely wasting my leisure time if I wished to describe them—I shall be content with a few, and indeed the most prominent ones. 

But lest anyone think that such narratives should be given little credit, let him hear that most certain decree and proclamation of Eunapius in his life of **Aedesius** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Iamblichus.</note>: he calls the guild of magicians the "divine assembly" <note>original: "diuinum cœtum"</note> and says they reject a lie and any speech that is not at all consistent with the truth. I will say nothing here of **Sosipatra** <note>A celebrated 4th-century female mystic and philosopher said to have possessed clairvoyant powers.</note>, whom Eunapius mentions in his account of Aedesius (and whom others mention as well), who for five whole years lived between two most impure Demons...

...with **incubi** <note>In folklore and demonology, male spirits said to haunt or have sexual relations with sleeping women.</note>, most filthily wallowing, and by them instructed in the more hidden mysteries of **Magic**; [I say] nothing of Antoninus her son, who, having traveled to Egypt, was initiated into sacred and secret mysteries and was quickly received into the companionship of **Demons**. **Maximus** and *Chrysanthius*, philosophers and teachers of the Emperor *Julian* the Apostate <note>Reigned 361–363 AD; the last pagan emperor of the Roman Empire, who famously attempted to roll back the influence of Christianity.</note> in magical matters, agreed perfectly among themselves and were occupied together in performing wondrous things through the appearance of divine, or rather demonic, inspiration, as *Eunapius* says in his life of *Maximus*.

But he [Maximus] especially, in the presence of *Julian* himself, demonstrated how powerful a <term>Magical</term> <term>Sacrifice</term> <note>original: "Magicum Sacrificium"</note> might be; in which, after *Maximus* himself had muttered some hymn or other to himself, the statue of the *Goddess* *Hecate* <note>An ancient Greek goddess associated with the crossroads, the moon, and witchcraft.</note>, in whose temple they were staying, began to smile—and that was indeed a true laugh—then a sudden flash of light ignited the torches. By which illusions *Julian* was so moved that, having kissed *Chrysanthius* (who had introduced him to *Maximus*), he betook himself to *Ephesus* to study under *Maximus* for the sake of learning. *Eunapius* records more concerning the magical sacrifices of this *Maximus*, both in his life of *Maximus* and in his life of *Chrysanthius*; he also records the magical arts of *Chrysanthius* himself...

-># MAGIC<-

[Eunapius] describes quite carefully how **Chrysanthius**, merely by gazing at the **Sun**, would pour forth true oracles, shaped into a double form of an inspired spirit by an infernal deity <note>original: "Numine infero"</note>; he then mentions the sacrifices repeated so often for the sake of **divination**. If I seem excessive to anyone in recounting these things, he will certainly grant me pardon when he considers that my purpose is to show how closely the duty of **sacrificing** is joined to the discipline of **Magic**, so much so that no one has ever bound themselves to the **devil** by <term>magical pacts</term> <note>A formal agreement between a person and a demonic entity, a central concept in early modern demonology.</note> without also sacrificing to him willingly and gladly. 

**Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a Christian historian and exegete.</note> emphasizes this quite often, especially in the *Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 5, chapter 6: that **demons** are honored with worship and adoration by magi and sorcerers. And certainly, in an old **Glossary** cited by **Turnebus** <note>Adrianus Turnebus (1512–1565), a renowned French classical scholar and chair of Greek at the Collège Royal.</note>—that other parent of more refined literature—a sorceress is called a *Tegularia* <note>From the Latin *tegula*, meaning a roof tile.</note>, because she sacrifices upon the roof tiles. 

From this, one may correct the **Glossary of Isidore** <note>St. Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), whose "Etymologies" served as the primary encyclopedia of the Middle Ages.</note>, in which it is written, "*Tegellaria venefica*" [a tile-poisoner/witch]; it should instead be read as *Tegularia*. And later, where it says, "*Tugellaria Milefitia, because they sacrifice upon the tile-like [surface]*," it should be read as: ***Tegularia Malefica*** <note>A "Roof-tile Sorceress."</note>, *because she sacrifices upon the roof tiles*.

# ON IDOLATRY

...[those who] pursue or practice the **magical arts**, I would say they rely on the same pact, are initiated by the same rite, and are bound by the same crime. Not all of them sacrifice, for there are indeed different classes and orders among them, of which we must first speak briefly, and then of the multiple kinds of sacrifices which that **primary deceiver** <note>A reference to the Devil; "veterator" literally implies a "sly old hand" or veteran in deception.</note> devised. He desired to be worshipped as God by **Pseudo-Christians**, whom he either mocked—if they were simpler and more unrefined—or cast the most base of men headlong into the deep abyss <term>barathrum</term> <note>A "barathrum" is a bottomless pit or chasm, used here metaphorically to describe the ultimate depths of sin and impiety.</note> of total impiety. 

It does not seem appropriate to review every single species of the magical art here; for, as I might say with **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) from Carthage, famous for his polemical works.</note> in his *Scorpiace* <note>A work titled "Antidote for the Scorpion's Sting," written to defend the necessity of martyrdom against Gnostic critics.</note>: "**There are as many poisons as there are kinds, as many destructions as species, as many pains as colors**" <note>original: "Tot venena, quot genera, tot pernicies quot ſpecies, tot dolores quot colores."</note>. These were all most diligently enumerated by **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot and polymath whose works categorized different forms of magic and spiritual communication.</note> in Book 1 of his *Antipalus* <note>The *Antipalus maleficiorum* (The Enemy of Witchcraft), a scholarly catalogue of magical practices.</note>, and before him by **Eymeric** <note>Nicholas Eymeric (c. 1320–1399), a Catalan Inquisitor who wrote the *Directorium Inquisitorum*, a manual for hunting heretics and sorcerers.</note> in the *Inquisitor's Directory*, chapter 43.

Truly, according to **Balsamon** <note>Theodore Balsamon (12th century), a prominent Byzantine jurist and commentator on canon law.</note>, among those Christians who practice **evil arts** <term>maleficas artes</term>, some—having renounced the name of Christ—acknowledge the devil as their supreme lord upon whom they entirely depend, having pledged their faith. Others, while preserving the Christian faith, [pursue] more curious...

# MAGIC

...out of anxiety, or under the pretext of some necessity, they explore that destructive art, as **Saint Gregory of Nyssa** <note>A 4th-century theologian and bishop (c. 335–395 AD), one of the "Cappadocian Fathers" who shaped early Christian doctrine.</note> writes in his *Canonical Epistle to Letoius*, chapter 3. All those of the first kind perform sacrifices to the devil.

**Balsamon** <note>Theodore Balsamon (c. 1140–1195 AD), the leading Byzantine expert on Church law, known for his commentaries on the canons.</note>, in his commentary on Canon 36 of the **Council of Laodicea** <note>A regional gathering of church leaders held around 363–364 AD which addressed many issues of discipline and liturgy.</note>, lists and explains them thus: those who approach **Satan** for some cause are called **Magi** <note>original: "Magi"</note>; **enchanters** are those who recite songs or incantations and by these means draw **demons** to their will. **Astrologers** are those who practice divination by the stars, with a demon providing them aid. He makes the class of **Mathematicians** <note>In the medieval and late antique world, "Mathematicians" often referred specifically to those who used astronomical calculations to cast horoscopes for divination.</note> distinct, though it has already been explained by many that they belong with the Astrologers.

Balsamon likewise identifies two kinds of these seers and diviners in his commentary on Canons 71 and 83 of **Saint Basil** <note>Basil of Caesarea (330–379 AD), an influential bishop whose rules for monks and penitents became law in the Eastern Church.</note>: for some do not renounce the faith of Christ, while others become **apostates** <note>Those who totally abandon their religious faith.</note> and completely and impiously fail in their faith. I believe it is these latter ones whom Balsamon means when he writes on Canon 61 of the **Council in Trullo** <note>An ecclesiastical council held in 692 AD in Constantinople; it is famous for issuing 102 canons regarding church discipline.</note> that seers or diviners, through sacrifices, interrogate their father **Satan** about certain matters and devote themselves entirely to the demon. Nor are only those who predict future things understood by this name of diviners...

Balsamon writes that those *Primicerii* seduce the simpler folk through their cursed rituals, yet they do not [share/communicate] those rituals...

# MAGICA.

...[he] mentions, which I believe were nothing other than responses from **Demons**, delivered in the form of some specter or similar apparition. According to the writers of **Byzantine histories**, the Greeks took great pleasure in these things, though this observation has already been made by others.

The same **Balsamon** <note>Theodore Balsamon (c. 1130–1195), a renowned specialist in Eastern Orthodox Church law and commentator on the canons.</note>, in his commentary on both cited Canons of **Blessed Basil** <note>St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), whose letters and rules became fundamental to Church discipline regarding magic.</note>, reports that some aged enchantresses deny God, while others do not; however, from his account, I have no doubt that both types invoke the Demon. We shall demonstrate this further when we discuss the various kinds of **magical sacrifices** <note>original: "Sacrificiorum magicorum".</note>.

**Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German Benedictine abbot and polymath. Though a monk, his writings on "natural magic" and cryptography often skirted the line of forbidden arts.</note>, an excessively diligent and curious investigator of magical matters, enumerates four types of evildoers <term>maleficorum; specifically those who use magic to cause harm</term> in the first book of his work, ***Antipalus*** <note>original: "Antipali". The full title is *Antipalus Maleficorum*, or *The Enemy of Sorcerers*, written in 1508.</note>.

The **first** type consists of those who, even if they have no manifest pact with Demons and do not use magical incantations <note>original: "carminibus magicis"; literally "magical songs" or chants.</note>, nevertheless accomplish many things by administering poisons, after which the most wicked works of Demons follow.

The **second** type includes those who, although they do not expressly invoke Demons, nevertheless...

# ON IDOLATRY

...[category consists of those who] use incantations to cause harm, and charms <term>præcantatione; specifically magical chants or spoken formulas used for healing</term> to heal: to this category he refers **Necromancy** <note>original: "Necromantiam"; the art of divining the future by communicating with the dead</note>, **Hydromancy** <note>original: "Hydromantiam"; divination performed by observing water</note> and similar arts.

The **third** category consists of those who, through an intervening pact, do not devote themselves as fully to the **Demon** as the fourth category does, yet nevertheless invoke **Demons** as collaborators in their wicked actions.

The **fourth** category belongs to those who not only have **Demons** as assistants like the others, but even—having renounced their **Baptism** and the **Christian faith**—surrender themselves to the law and power of the **Demon** by a wicked type of profession <note>In this context, a "profession" is a formal declaration or vow, similar to a monk's profession but dedicated to evil</note>.

**Trithemius** piles up many claims here that can neither be proven nor defended, and we shall investigate them later. But whatever he may say, we will easily demonstrate from **Trithemius** himself that, with the exception of the evildoers of the first category, all the rest employ sacrifices. As for those of the first category, they offer a silent worship to the **Demon**, or rather an open one, if we believe **Eymeric** <note>Nicolas Eymeric (c. 1320–1399), a Spanish Inquisitor whose manual, the *Directorium Inquisitorum*, defined the Church's legal approach to sorcery</note>, as we have previously noted.

I pass over that peculiar class of utterly accursed men who, by means of wicked pacts and written records—and even with witnesses <note>original: "obſignatoribus"; literally "sealers," those who sign or witness a legal document</note> summoned from the **underworld** <note>original: "ex Orco"; referring to Orcus, a Roman deity of the underworld, used here as a synonym for Hell</note>—present themselves as companions to **Demons**, whether **succubi** or **incubi** <note>Demons believed to take female and male forms respectively to engage in sexual acts with humans</note>.

# MAGIC

I know that many mock that shameful lust <note>original: "propudiosam istam venerem"; here referring to the carnal relations between humans and demons</note>, but let them at least hear **Bede** <note>Saint Bede the Venerable (c. 672–735), an English monk and scholar whose works were central to medieval theology</note>, a man worthy of both veneration and trust. In his **Commentary on Luke**, Book 3, Chapter 8, he writes the following on this matter, after having discussed how some are possessed by a **Demon** who takes on the role of either an **incubus** <term>incubus; a male demon believed to have sexual intercourse with sleeping women</term> or a **succubus** <term>succubus; a female demon believed to have sexual intercourse with sleeping men</term>. He explains that in exorcisms, the **Demon** is forced to reveal the methods of renouncing the pact of "love" that a man or woman has struck with such a **succubus** or **incubus**.

> *This matter, says **Bede**, seems very much like a lie, yet it is so true that it is extremely well known through the testimony of many people.*

However, these matters are not the focus of our current project, and they have been most certainly demonstrated by many others. And surely, since the laws of the **Emperors** encompass under the name of "**Magicians**" all those whom we have reviewed above, they also testify that these people celebrate demonic **Sacrifices**. See the **Theodosian Code** <note>The *Codex Theodosianus*, a collection of Roman laws compiled in the 5th century</note>, the title concerning **evildoers and astrologers** <note>original: "de maleficis et mathematicis"; in Roman law, 'mathematicians' were often synonymous with astrologers and diviners</note>, law 7:

> *We decree by eternal authority that no one shall henceforth attempt to celebrate wicked prayers, or magical preparations, or deadly sacrifices during the nighttime; those discovered and convicted shall be punished with the appropriate sentence.*

The **Interpretation** <note>The *Interpretatio* refers to the simplified summaries provided alongside the complex legal language of the Theodosian Code</note> attached to the law states:

> *Whoever celebrates nocturnal sacrifices to demons, or by incantations [summons] demons...*

# ON IDOLATRY

...[invoked] <note>original: "nocauerit", likely "invocauerit"</note>, he shall be punished with death. **Paulus** <note>Julius Paulus (3rd century AD), an influential Roman jurist whose writings became a primary source for later Roman law</note> in the same place, in the fifth book of his *Opinions*, title 23: ***Those who have performed, or arranged for the performance of, impious or nocturnal rites in order to bewitch or bind someone <note>original: "obcantarent, defigerent"; "defigerent" refers to the practice of 'binding' a victim through a curse tablet or "defixio"</note>, shall either be crucified or thrown to the beasts.*** By a Decree of the Senate, those "evil sacrifices" are condemned which **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), a prominent Roman jurist whose works make up about a third of the later Justinian Digest</note> mentions in the *Digest* <term>ff.; an abbreviation for the "Digest" or "Pandects," a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions</term> under the **Cornelian Law concerning Assassins and Poisoners** <term>lex Cornelia de sicariis et veneficis; a law established in 81 BC that became the primary legal instrument for prosecuting magic in Rome</term>, law 13. The legal experts explain these as nothing other than **Magical** rites, and I have no doubt that those nocturnal gatherings of **witches** <term>sagae; female practitioners of magic, often associated with prophecy or malevolence</term> and **evildoers** <term>malefici; those who use "maleficium" or harmful magic</term> are being described. 

Now, let us weigh what kind of sacrifice is offered to the **devil** by **magicians** and **evildoers**. We observe a sacrifice of the first kind, which consists of physical things that can be perceived by the senses, and these are indeed of many sorts. For some are produced from the earth, such as flowers and plants, like frankincense and similar things. Others are sought from man himself, as when someone devotes either himself to the **Demon** as a most pleasing victim, or devotes another human being. 

The second kind occurs when the elements through which the sacrifice is completed are perceived by no one except the **Magician** or the **evildoer** himself; just as the **devil** knows that a sacrifice consisting of pure prayer is acceptable to God, so he demands that sacrifices be made to him through silent prayers and hymns. In a word, it is either bodily...

### MAGIC

...[bloody] <note>original: "reum"; likely "sanguineum" (bloody) or "corporeum" (corporeal), completing the thought from the previous page</note> sacrifice, or a spiritual one. Let us now explain each one in detail. There is no need for me to recount how, even after the birth of Christ, living creatures were slaughtered and immolated to the devil among the **Barbarians** living in either the **East Indies** or the **New World** <note>a 16th/17th-century term for the Americas</note>. However, those who boast that they are **Christians**, even if they do not enter into an express pact with the **Demon**, nevertheless do not dare to evoke a Demon to ask for something without some mode and rite of **Sacrifice**. Instead, they employ certain selected herbs and plants; and, what is the core of this mystical and wicked rite, they always use **incense** <note>original: "thure"</note>. We shall demonstrate without difficulty that this is a most certain proof of a Sacrifice, of whatever kind it may be.

**Arnobius** <note>Arnobius of Sicca (d. c. 330 AD), an Early Christian apologist who wrote to defend the faith against pagan accusations</note> in book 7 of *Against the Heathens* <note>original: "Adversus Gentes"</note> writes: ***Without incense, the duty of religion limps*** <note>original: "Sine thure religionis officium claudicat"</note>. He is indicating that in that **Ethnic** <note>pagan or non-Christian</note> superstition, the Sacrifice upon which religion rests cannot be performed in any way without incense. 

**Optatus of Milevis** <note>A 4th-century Bishop in North Africa known for his writings against the Donatists</note> in his third book records two formulas that the **Ethnics** <term>Ethnici; a term used here for pagans or those practicing non-Christian Roman religions</term> used when harrassing Christians and driving them toward **Apostasy** <term>Apostasia; the total desertion or abandonment of one's religion</term>: ***Either offer incense, or destroy the Basilicas*** <note>original: "Aut thus pone, aut Basilicas dirue"</note>. This latter part I will not explain now, as it is not relevant to this topic, though he calls it by other names...
