# ON IDOLATRY

...that is, to subvert the Temples of the living God; and he repeats the same sentiment: "Offer incense," which he explains thus:

> *The war declared against Christians was raging; the devil triumphs in the temples of demons; altars smoked with filthy stenches <note>original: "nidoribus"; specifically the smell of burning fat or meat</note>, and whoever was unable to come to the sacrilegious rites was compelled to offer incense wherever they were. Every place was a Temple for this crime.*

From these things, we understand that Christians appeared to the **Heathens** <note>original: "Ethnicis"; referring to non-Christian practitioners of Roman religion</note> to have sacrificed to their Gods if they had merely placed a grain of incense on the **incense-casket** <note>original: "acerræ"</note>. Saint **Jerome** <note>a prominent 4th-century scholar and Church Father</note>, in his first Letter to **Heliodorus**, illustrates the rite in this way:

> *It is no small service to an idol if someone throws incense onto the burning altar with two little fingers.*

And the **Sacrificers** <note>original: "sacrificati"</note>, whom Saint **Cyprian** <note>a 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage who wrote extensively on those who faltered during Roman persecution</note> mentions many times, also included those who had merely thrown incense. For he identifies only two categories of those who had faltered in the faith of Christ: some were **Libellatics** <note>original: "Libellaticos"; those who obtained a certificate—a "libellus"—stating they had sacrificed to the Roman gods, often to avoid actually having to do so</note>, who sinned more lightly, and others were **Sacrificers**, who had sinned more gravely (Epistle 52, to Antonianus). Certainly, **Optatus** himself, near the end of his second book against [the Donatists] <note>The Latin text here reads "contra Optatum," which is likely a printer's error for "contra Donatum" or "contra Parmenianum"</note>, used the term "to offer incense" <note>original: "thurificare"</note> in place of "to sacrifice" <note>original: "sacrificare"</note> in a general sense; not because every sacrifice consisted only of incense, but because nothing could be offered to the Gods either more frequently or more pleasingly.

## ON IDOLATRY

...Christians who had fallen into **idolatry**, it was said "to make a fire" and "to set out incense" <note>original: "Thus"</note> instead of "to sacrifice." But indeed, in magical rites—whether one deals with a demon by an express pact, or by a silent one and through mere evocations—**Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius, 1462–1516, a Benedictine abbot and famous scholar of cryptography and the occult</note>, who was knowledgeable in these matters, writes that incense is employed. <note>This poem warns that the devil’s greatest trick is not making people evil, but making them think they are doing something holy while they are actually worshipping him.</note>

> "Blessed are you, Lord Abraham; truly I say you are blessed, because no one among all who act rightly has been found like you, and no one is so [entirely...]"

By this desire, he strives by all possible means to have whatever constitutes divine worship and veneration shown to himself alone by these idle, wicked men, or by others if he is able. But since the question now proposed concerns the punishments with which sorcerers <term>Sorcerers: In this context, *malefici*, those who use "evil-doing" or harmful magic.</term> seem fit to be afflicted, I will resolve it with the clearest demonstration. Either these individuals merely know the magical art, or they put it into practice—and this in two ways: either by harming another, or by helping. 

The first case—the mere knowledge—cannot be free from guilt, since by the consensus of all laws, divine and human, magic is condemned. Nor should it be without penalty if someone has learned it, just as he who teaches that art cannot deservedly escape punishment. For there is an equal crime in learning and teaching things profane and forbidden (*Code on Heretics*, Law 2 <note>original: *Cod. de Hæret. l. 2* — referring to the Code of Justinian, a fundamental collection of Roman law.</note>). The third type is those, clearly, who completely deny that they are sorcerers; yet while they bring aid to the sick, they employ remedies which were neither known to nor approved by many of the ancient physicians <term>Physicians: *Medici* — referring here to the classical medical authorities like Galen or Hippocrates, whose works formed the basis of Renaissance medicine.</term>. These remedies, by their very nature, possess absolutely no power or energy for healing such diseases, and they differ from legitimate medicine by the widest possible margin. 

For it is neither written nor said anywhere among medical writers that a steel mirror <note>original: *Speculum chalybeum* — a polished steel surface used in "catoptromancy," or divination by mirrors.</note> can cure a hernia. Those who think so deceive and are themselves deceived...

# MAGIC

# ON IDOLATRY

...or that a sorcerer is rare, and the so-called wizards and witches have no commerce at all with the devil; and if they are said to have harmed crops or grapes, we pronounce it to be an error of the common people. If we hear that horses, oxen, sheep, and other living creatures were suddenly struck—even though the sky was clear and calm just a moment before—by heavy thunder, a storm from the stars, or by the lightning of Jupiter himself <note>original: "Diali fulmine" — Referring to Diespiter or Jupiter, the Roman god of the sky and thunder.</note>, and sometimes by a hidden sorcery <term>maleficium</term> <note>Maleficium refers specifically to "wrongdoing" or "mischief" performed through magic intended to cause harm.</note>, we attribute it to mere chance. Regarding the conjuring of rain, hail, and similar things, we declare with Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Stoic philosopher who often argued against superstitious interpretations of natural phenomena.</note> that it is a myth; why should we not also, with Seneca in his book *On Consolation to Marcia* and in Epistles 24 and 82, mock the Underworld and the torments of the dead?

> *Those things,* he says, *which make the Underworld terrifying to us, are a myth. Poets have toyed with those things and disturbed us with empty fears. Death is both the release and the end of all pains, beyond which our evils do not go.*

Indeed, we might add: now there is no God except the one each person has fashioned for themselves; for this alone remains from that sentiment of Seneca to reach the highest level of impiety. What if some people fall under suspicion of having committed those crimes, and are therefore brought to trial? There are not a few, nor light, nor ob—

<meta>catchword: scura, sed (obscure, but)</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the skeptical view that magic and diabolical influence are merely common superstitions, using the philosopher Seneca as a representative of those who dismiss the supernatural as poetic myth.</summary>

### MAGICA.

...scure, but rather innumerable, most certain, and far clearer arguments and testimonies than the "Rhodian Sun" <note>original: "Sol Rhodius" — A proverbial expression for something exceptionally bright or obvious, as the island of Rhodes was famously sunny.</note> are what we require. So far, we have enumerated many things from which we may, not without reason, hold someone to be a Mage or a sorcerer <term>maleficus</term> <note>A practitioner of harmful magic intended to cause injury or misfortune.</note>, if these things are supported to some extent by the open testimonies of men worthy of trust. Sacrifices offered to the Demon, meetings held, and sacred objects—specifically of that kind which it is a sacrilege for the uninitiated to touch—that are kept and discovered in their homes, are quite compelling evidence.

I add to this unknown characters <note>Magical sigils or symbols used in occult rituals.</note> and images, and even "Acherontic books" <note>original: "libros Acheronticos" — Books of the Underworld; Acheron was one of the rivers of Hades in Greek mythology.</note>, for so Arnobius <note>Arnobius of Sicca (d. c. 330 AD), an Early Christian apologist who wrote against pagan practices.</note> calls those magical books in his second book. Those who sell such things are certainly worthy of being enrolled in a "colony of galleys" <note>original: "triremium coloniam" — A figurative way of saying they should be sentenced to hard labor as galley slaves on ships.</note>.

Does anyone, therefore, doubt that we are those upon whom the "ends of the ages have come," as the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in 1 Corinthians 10:11.</note> says? And I can hardly doubt that Satan has been released from those chains of his, or that he is very near to being released; it is clearly certain to me, since I see few who wish for God’s kingdom to be strengthened and Satan’s kingdom to be overthrown. Indeed, I see that Satan’s own clients, followers, and servants are regarded as innocent and harmless within the Christian world. But whoever you are, who are both an admirer and a scrut— <meta>catchword: perſcru- (scrutinizer/investigator)</meta>

K

<summary>The author argues that physical evidence like magical symbols, forbidden books, and pagan sacrifices proves the reality of sorcery, and laments that society has become too lenient toward those he considers servants of Satan in the end times.</summary>

<header>ON MAGICAL IDOLATRY.</header>

...reader, if by chance you should come across these writings of mine, stay a while. Place on one side Satan, whom you dearly love, and on the other Christ, whom you seem to reject. Does your mind waver, and is it not clear enough to you whom you should follow? Well done, "Christian," <note>The author uses "Well done" (Eugè) ironically here to challenge the reader's wavering faith.</note>

> *The wind is favorable here now; just take up the steering-oar,* <note>original: "vorsoriam" — a nautical term for a rope or oar used to turn a ship; here it metaphorically refers to changing one's course in life.</note>
> *This is the serene West wind; that other is the rainy South:*
> *This one brings tranquility; that other stirs up all the waves.* <note>These lines are adapted from the Roman playwright Plautus, using maritime metaphors to contrast the peace of Christ with the chaos of the devil.</note>

Dismiss the messenger of that apostate <note>Referring to Satan, the "Apostate" who rebelled against God.</note> if you are wise; enough of this has been done so far by your evil arts and incantations, and by our own most heavy sins.

*We have made the heavens guilty.* <note>original: "Fecimus cælum nocens" — A quote from Seneca’s tragedy *Oedipus*, suggesting that human wickedness is so great it pollutes the very sky and brings divine plague.</note>

# ARTICLES OF THE THEOLOGIANS
## Of Paris, concerning Magic and the 
## invocation of Demons.
### *From the treatise of Jean Gerson, Chancellor of Paris, "On errors regarding the Magical art."* <note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429) was a prominent French scholar and reformer who wrote extensively on the boundaries between science, superstition, and faith.</note>
#### *First Article.*

That seeking familiarity, friendship, and the help of Demons through Magical arts, sorceries <term>maleficia</term> <note>Acts of harmful magic.</note>, and wicked invocations is not Idolatry. **[This is an] Error.** <note>Gerson presents a statement commonly used to justify magic and then labels it a theological "Error" to be condemned.</note>

<meta>Ink smudge at bottom right corner potentially obscuring catchword or signature.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the author's personal appeal to the reader to reject magic and begins a formal list of theological condemnations against sorcery issued by Jean Gerson and the University of Paris.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Gerson, Paris Theologians, Satan vs. Christ, idolatry, nautical metaphors, Seneca, theological errors, sorcery</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Articles of the Theologians of Paris" concerning magical errors, specifically finishing the reasoning for the first article (begun on the previous page) and listing articles two through four.</meta>

<sig>K ij</sig>
<meta>Catchword or ink smudge at bottom left corner.</meta>

...because the Demon is judged to be an adversary, persistent and implacable, toward both God and man. Nor is he capable of receiving any true honor or dominion—neither in reality, nor by participation, nor even by suitability—as other rational creatures who are not damned might, nor through a sign established by human will, such as images and temples, for God is honored in those things. <note>The argument here is that while we can honor holy objects like temples because they point to God, one cannot "honor" a demon because they are inherently incapable of receiving legitimate honor.</note>

**Art. 2.** That to give, offer, or promise any thing whatsoever to Demons so that they might fulfill a person’s desire, or to kiss or carry something in their honor, is not Idolatry. **Error.** <note>The word "Error" marks a proposition that the University of Paris officially condemned as heretical or false.</note>

**Art. 3.** That entering into a pact with Demons—whether silent or expressed—is not Idolatry or a form of Idolatry and <term>Apostasy</term> <note>Apostasy: The total rejection or abandonment of one's religious faith.</note>. **Error.** And we understand a pact to be implicit in every superstitious practice whose result cannot reasonably be expected from God or nature. <note>The theologians argue that if you perform a ritual that has no natural or divine explanation, you are making an "implicit pact" with the devil even if you don't say it out loud.</note>

**Art. 4.** That it is not Idolatry to use <term>Magical arts</term> <note>original: "artes Magicas" — refers specifically to ritual or ceremonial magic intended to command spirits.</note> to enclose, compel, or constrain Demons within stones, rings, mirrors, or images consecrated—or rather, <term>execrated</term> <note>original: "execratis" — a play on "consecrated." To execrate is to declare something cursed or abominable.</note>—in their name, or to wish to bring those images to life. **Error.**

**Art. 5.** That it is permitted to perform certain actions for any good purpose through magical arts or other superstitions prohibited by God or by the Church. **Error.**
Because according to the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in the New Testament (Romans 3:8), a foundational rule in Christian ethics stating that the ends do not justify the means.</note>: 
> Evil must not be done so that good may come of it. <note>original: "Non sunt facienda mala, vt inde eueniant bona."</note>

**Art. 6.** That it is lawful, and even should be permitted, to repel sorcery with sorcery <note>original: "maleficia maleficijs repellere" — this refers to the common practice of seeking a "counter-spell" or a "cunning man" to undo a curse.</note>. **Error.**

**Art. 7.** That anyone can grant a dispensation <note>A formal exemption from a religious law or vow.</note> to another in any case whatsoever, so that they may lawfully use such things. **Error.**

**Art. 8.** That the <term>Magical arts</term> <note>original: "artes Magicæ"</note> and similar superstitions and their practices have been irrationally prohibited by the Church. **Error.**

**Art. 9.** That God is induced through magical arts and sorceries to compel demons to obey those who invoke them. **Error.**

**Art. 10.** That the <term>thurifications</term> <note>original: "thurificationes" — the ritual burning of incense.</note> and <term>suffumigations</term> <note>original: "suffumigationes" — the use of ritual smoke or vapors.</note> which are performed in the practice of such arts and sorceries are to the honor of God and are pleasing to Him. **Error and blasphemy:** for otherwise God would not prohibit or punish them.

**Art. 11.** That to use such things in such a way is not a Sacrifice, or an offering to Demons—and consequently, is not a damnable act of <term>Idolatry</term> <note>original: "idolatrare" — the theologians argue that any ritual interaction with demons, even if labeled "prayer," is functionally a sacrifice to a false god.</note>. **Error.**

**Art. 12.** That holy words and certain devoted prayers, fasts, ritual washings, and bodily chastity in boys and others: and the celebration of Masses, and other works of a good nature, which are performed for the exercise of such arts, excuse those who do them from evil rather than accuse them. **Error:** for through such things sacred objects, and indeed God Himself in the <term>Eucharist</term> <note>The central Christian sacrament of Holy Communion. The text is concerned that magicians were incorporating the consecrated Host into their spells.</note> is tempted to be sacrificed to Demons; the Demon procures this because he wishes to be honored in this way similarly to the <term>Most High</term> <note>original: "Altiffimo" — a name for God, emphasizing the demon's pride in wanting to be treated as God's equal.</note>: either to hide his frauds, or so that he may ensnare the simple-minded more easily, and destroy them more damnably.

**Art. 13.** That the holy Prophets and others obtained their Prophecies and performed miracles, or expelled Demons, through such arts. **Error and blasphemy.**

**Art. 14.** That it is possible through such arts to compel the <term>free will</term> <note>original: "liberum arbitriũ" — the capacity of a human being to make choices without divine or demonic coercion.</note> of a person toward the will or desire of another. **Error**, and to attempt to do so is impious and wicked.

**Art. 15.** That the aforementioned arts are good for this reason, and are from God, such that it is permitted to observe them: because through them occasionally, or even frequently, it happens just as those using them seek, or <note>The text ends mid-sentence as this is the bottom of the page; it likely continues to discuss how the apparent "success" of a spell does not prove its holiness.</note>

**Art. 16.** That by such arts Demons are truly forced and compelled: and that they do not instead pretend to be so compelled in order to seduce men. **Error.** <note>The Church argued that a human cannot actually control a demon; the demon only acts "forced" to trick the magician into further sin.</note>

**Art. 17.** That by such arts and impious rites, by <term>sorceries</term> <note>original: "sortilegia" — literally "the casting of lots," a form of divination.</note>, by <term>incantations</term> <note>original: "carminationes" — the chanting of magical songs or verses.</note>, by the invocation of Demons, by certain <term>effigy-makings</term> <note>original: "inuultationes" — the practice of creating wax or clay images of people to influence or harm them (similar to "voodoo dolls").</note>, and other <term>evil deeds</term> <note>original: "maleficia" — harmful magic intended to cause injury or misfortune.</note>, no effect ever follows through the ministry of Demons. **Error.** For God sometimes allows such things to happen, as was evident in the <term>Magicians of Pharaoh</term> <note>A reference to Exodus 7, where Pharaoh's magicians replicated some of Moses's miracles through their secret arts.</note>, and elsewhere many times: either for the testing of the faithful, as is found in <term>Deuteronomy 13</term> <note>A biblical passage warning that even if a false prophet’s sign comes true, it may be a test from God.</note>; or as a worthy scourging of certain men; or because those who misuse these things or consult them—on account of bad faith or other nefarious sins—are given over to a <term>depraved mind</term> <note>original: "reprobum sensum" — a spiritual state where one can no longer distinguish right from wrong.</note>, and deserve to be thus deceived.

**Art. 18.** That good Angels can be confined in stones, and may consecrate images or garments, or do other things contained in these arts. **Error and blasphemy.**

**Art. 19.** That the blood of a <term>hoopoe</term> <note>original: "vpupæ" — a bird frequently cited in medieval grimoires for its supposed magical properties.</note> or of a <term>kid</term> <note>original: "hœdi" — a young goat.</note>, or of another animal, or parchmen...

...virgin parchment <note>original: "virgineum" — parchment made from the skin of a stillborn or neonatal animal, considered "pure" for ritual use.</note>, or the skin of a lion and similar things, have the power to compel or repel Demons through the service of such arts. **Error.**

*Art. 20.* That images made of bronze or of lead, or of white or red wax, or of other materials—whether baptized or exorcised, consecrated or rather cursed—according to the aforementioned arts and on certain [astrological] days, possess the miraculous powers that are described in the books of such arts. **Error in faith, in natural Philosophy, and in true Astrology.** <note>The Church here distinguishes "true" astrology (the study of physical celestial influence) from "superstitious" magic that uses physical icons to trap or channel spiritual power.</note>

*Art. 21.* That to use such things and to put faith in them is not idolatry and unfaithfulness. **Error.**

*Art. 22.* That some Demons are good: some are kind, others are all-knowing, and others are neither saved nor damned. **Error.** <note>Orthodox theology maintained that all demons were fallen angels who were irrevocably evil and condemned to hell.</note>

*Art. 23.* That the <term>fumigations</term> <note>original: "fumigationes" — the practice of burning incense, herbs, or other substances during a ritual to attract or satisfy spirits.</note> which are performed in such operations are transformed into spirits, or that such things are owed to them as a right. **Error.**

*Art. 24.* That one Demon is the King of the East—and especially so by his own merit—another is King of the West, another of the North, and another of the South. **Error.** <note>A direct condemnation of the "Four Kings" system found in medieval <term>grimoires</term> (magic manuals), which assigned a demonic ruler to each cardinal direction.</note>

*Art. 25.* That the <term>moving intelligence</term> <note>original: "intelligentia motrix" — in medieval cosmology, these were spiritual beings (often identified as angels) believed to move the celestial spheres and planets.</note> of the heavens flows into the rational soul, just as...

...[the influence of] the celestial body upon the human body. **Error.** <note>This likely completes Article 25, which argued that stars or planets could physically compel human actions, a concept that threatened the doctrine of free will.</note>

***Art. 26.*** That our intellectual thoughts and inner volitions <note>original: "volitiones" — acts of the will or choices.</note> are immediately judged or determined by the heavens, and that through some magical tradition, such thoughts can be known, and that it is lawful to judge with certainty about them through that tradition. **Error.** <note>The Church maintained that only God can know the secret thoughts of the heart; the stars have no power over the soul.</note>

***Art. 27.*** That through any magical arts whatsoever we can arrive at a vision of the divine essence, or of the holy spirits. **Error.** <note>This condemns "theurgy" or high magic, which claimed that rituals could lead a practitioner to see God or angels directly, bypassing the established sacraments of the Church.</note>

These things were enacted and—after mature and frequent examination between us and our deputies—concluded in our general congregation: at Paris, at the Church of Saint Mathurin <note>The traditional meeting place for the Faculty of Theology at the University of Paris.</note>, in the morning, having been specifically convened for this purpose. In the year of our Lord, one thousand three hundred ninety-eight, on the nineteenth day of September.

# **The External Binding**

->[UN]FRUITFUL<- <note>original: "FEI," a fragment of the word *Infecondi* (The Unfruitful). This refers to the **Accademia degli Infecondi** of Rome, a literary society founded in the mid-17th century. Members of such academies often chose names suggesting humility or "emptiness" to contrast with the intellectual "fruit" they hoped to produce.</note>

->· D · G · D · M · M · A · H · P · P · M · I · G · P · C · L · A ·<- <note>These initials likely represent a formal string of titles or a specific dedicatory phrase belonging to a high-ranking member or patron of the Academy. In the context of Roman heraldry, such abbreviations often refer to aristocratic lineages or official positions within the Papal administration.</note>

->THINGS TURNED OVER BRING FERTILITY<- <note>original: "REVOLVTA FOECVNDANT." This is the central motto of the Academy. It explains the imagery on the shield below it: just as a field must be ploughed—the soil "turned over"—before it can bear a harvest, the mind must be exercised through study and "revolved" in thought to produce wisdom.</note>

<summary>This page describes the ornate red morocco leather binding of the manuscript, featuring the gold-tooled emblem of the Roman Academy of the Unfruitful. The motto and imagery of the ploughed field serve as an allegory for the necessity of hard intellectual labor.</summary>

<keywords>bookbinding, heraldry, Accademia degli Infecondi, Roman academies, Latin motto, gold tooling, intellectual labor, symbolism</keywords>
